   1|                                              loved and will be forever in his family's hearts and minds.
   2|                                         Helen D. "Denise" Galloway, 65, June 10, 2019
   3|                                         For over 23 years, Denise worked for the Lunenburg Public Schools. She worked in the
   4|                                         library at Turkey Hill and was the administrative secretary at the former T.C. Passios and
   5|                                         Lunenburg Primary. To many, Denise was the first person parents spoke with when
   6|                                         enrolling their children in school. She will be remembered for her kind heart and the way
   7|                                         she could reassure even the most nervous new students. She greeted everyone with a
   8|                                         warm and welcoming smile. To all who knew and loved Denise, she will be deeply
   9|                                         missed.
  10|                                          Catherine "Catie" Keefer, 40 June 14, 2019
  11|                                         Catie along with her sister owned and operated a daycare in Lunenburg for several years.
  12|                                         She was a lifeguard and swim instructor at the Lunenburg Town Beach in the summer
  13|                                         months. She was also very active in Lunenburg Youth Soccer for many years as a coach
  14|                                         and a parent. Some of her favorite things were going to the ocean whenever possible, her
  15|                                         lime green Jeep Wrangler, annual trips to Disney World and Gettysburg,PA. The most
  16|                                         important people to her were her two sons, and her family.
  17|                                         Janice P. (Davies) Carrier ,75, September 9, 2019
  18|                                         Jan worked as the Administrative Assistant for the Conservation Commission. She retired
  19|                                         from the position that she loved in December 2015. Jan loved her community and
  20|                                         demonstrated that through her service to the same. Whether serving on building
  21|                                         committees, the Cemetery Commission, the Historical Society, the Caucus Committee or
  22|                                         any of several other groups, she always had something to give. She was a dedicated
  23|                                         member and former secretary of the Lunenburg Turkey Hills Family Lions Club. She
  24|                                         served as Lunenburg's Election Warden for over 45 years. and was usually the first face
  25|                                         you would see in the lobby upon entering to cast your ballot. Jan enjoyed spending time
  26|                                         out and about with friends and family. Her children and grandchildren meant the world to
  27|                                         her.
  28|                                               Patrick Michael Barney, 41, October 5, 2019
  29|                                               Patrick was appointed as a Reserve Officer at the Lunenburg Police Department
  30|                                               July 1, 2000 and became a full-time Officer on January 13, 2002. He was a Squad
  31|                                               Leader when he graduated from the Massachusetts Police Corps. He always hoped
  32|                                               that Lunenburg would initiate a canine force as he loved animals, especially his
  33|                                               dachshund, Sable. After a critical incident in 2006, Pat developed PTSD and as a
  34|                                               result retired from the Police Department on August 8, 2012. Patrick then lived
  35|                                               between Massachusetts and Florida. Patrick had a joyous smile, infectious laugh and
  36|                                               was always willing to help others. Even in his death, he donated his organs so that
  37|                                               others could enjoy life

   1|                                                           FY' 19 TAX RATE-$18.68
   2|                     OPEN TOWN MEETING GOVERNMENT- FIVE MEMBER BOARD OF SELECTMEN
   3|                                        ANNUAL TOWN MEETING- IST SATURDAY IN MAY
   4|                                       ANNUAL TOWN ELECTION- 3RD SATURDAY IN MAY
   5|                                                        AREA- 26.63 SQUARE MILES
   6|                                                          ROAD MILES- ABOUT 100
   7|                    Lunenburg was settled in 1718 and incorporated in 1728. It occupies a land area of
   8|                    approximately 26.4 square miles. Lunenburg is located in north central Massachusetts
   9|                    approximately 43 miles from Boston. It is bordered on the west by the Town of Ashby and
  10|                    the City of Fitchburg, on the south by the Town of Lancaster and the City of Leominster, on
  11|                    the east by the Town of Shirley, and on the north by the Town of Townsend.
  12|                    Local legislative decisions are made by an open town meeting consisting of all registered voters in the town. Subject to the legislative decisions made by town meeting, the affairs of
  13|                     the town are generally administered by a five member Board of Selectmen and a Town
  14|                    Commit wait focal at as are and by a they a dead on means alo appointed on an at-large basis for staggered three-year terms.
  15|                     Lunenburg is a semi-rural community located in northern Worcester County. Three water bodies account for somewhat of a clustered approach to the town's historical development
  16|                     Lake Whalom and the former Whalom Amusement Park delineated the Whalom District
  17|                    from the remainder of Lunenburg. This district was developed as a housing base for many
  18|                    factory workers working in the mills and factories of Leominster and Fitchburg. In many
  19|                     ways the characteristics of the Whalom District more closely reflect characteristics of these
  20|                     cities than of the rest of Lunenburg. The Hickory Hills area of town, around Hickory Hills
  21|                     third body of water is Shirley Reservoir, a public water body also developed with manly Lake, was developed as a private community comprised largely of vacation cottages. ne
  22|                     vacation cottages that have now been modified to year-round residences. Commercial and
  23|                    industrial development is permitted in several areas of town, primarily along the two major
  24|                     arteries (Routes 2A and 13), and the town's peripheries, due largely to the close proximity of
  25|                     Leominster's and Fitchburg's sewer and water systems. Although many farms and orchards
  26|                     had slowly developed into single family housing, farming continues on in Lunenburg.
  27|                                                                           7

===PAGE 21=== (ocr, 2 lines)
   1|                                                   ADMINISTRATION
   2|                                                                          19

===PAGE 23=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|                   and Board of Selectmen continue to work hard on serving the best interests of the Town and
   2|                   providing a high level of services while remaining fiscally responsible to the taxpayers.
   3|                   During 2019 there were many topics addressed and accomplishments to highlight:
   4|                  • As part of the annual goal setting process, the Selectmen reviewed their goals from FY19
   5|                       and formalized their goals for FY20. The goals the Board identified for FY2019 included
   6|                       identifying, defining, discussing, and finalizing the criteria to be used in determining the
   7|                       use and/or re-use of the Town Hall, Ritter, old Primary, Brooks House, and TC Passios and
   8|                       to propose at least one project to the 2020 Annual Town Meeting regarding the use and/or
   9|                       reuse of the old Primary School. The Board identified the criteria used in determining the
  10|                       use and/or re-use of the aforementioned buildings in the fall of 2019 and discussed the
  11|                       future use of the old Primary throughout the year and continued to narrow down the focus
  12|                       for the future use of this building into 2020.
  13|                   • The Town Manager's goals included developing and implementing steps towards the
  14|                       redevelopment/reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, implementation of a recycling
  15|                       contamination enforcement program, and planning/implementing accessibility
  16|                       improvements.
  17|                   • There were multiple projects that addressed accessibility at various town facilities, town
  18|                       property, and public rights of way throughout the year. In January the Town Manager
  19|                       announced receipt of an ADA Project Grant from MA Office on Disability for $7,500 for
  20|                       accessibility improvements to the Lane, Cowdrey, and Small Town Forest properties. Also
  21|                       in January the Veterans Service Office was relocated from the Eagle House to the Ritter
  22|                       Memorial Building to be more accessible for veterans as the previous office was located on
  23|                       a second floor without a handicap lift so the Veterans Officer was relocated to the Ritter
  24|                       Memorial Building lower level that has a lift. The Town Manager coordinated the use of
  25|                       Montachusett Technical students to renovate the second floor Town Hall restroom to
  26|                       become a handicapped accessible bathroom and this was started in February and completed
  27|                       by May. In March the Town Manager applied for and ultimately received a District Local
  28|                       Technical Assistance grant to update the Town's ADA Self-Evaluation and Transition
  29|                        Plan. The Town was one of three communities that participated in a Public Rights of Way
  30|                       study through our regional planning agency that used a consultant to review the town's
  31|                       sidewalks, ramps, and other ADA requirements. Around June the handicapped parking
  32|                       spot at Town Hall was moved from an uneven brick sidewalk to a concrete level spot, the
  33|                       chairlift door at Town Hall was fixed, and the uneven granite steps at the Ritter Memorial
  34|                        Building were repaired.
  35|                                                                          21

===PAGE 24=== (ocr, 34 lines)
   1|                        of the program, the town's contamination rate was cut by twenty percent.
   2|                    • The redevelopment and/or reuse of 925 Massachusetts Avenue, an old vacant gas station
   3|                        with potential environmental hazards, has been a property the Town has sought to clean-
   4|                        up for over a decade but due to the unknown environmental hazards have not taken the
   5|                        property for back taxes so that the onus of the potential clean-up costs was not borne by
   6|                        the Town. After much research and consultation with the State and legal counsel, we were
   7|                        able to gain an Administrative Search Warrant that gave the Town the ability to apply for
   8|                        grant funding to perform an environmental assessment and right to access the property for
   9|                        the inspection and sampling needed. In September the Court granted the Administrative
  10|                        Search Warrant and the Town Manager submitted a Brownfields Site Assessment Grant
  11|                        through Montachusett Regional Planning Agency for the costs of the environmental site
  12|                        assessment, which was approved. The environmental site assessment is expected to be
  13|                        complete by the summer of 2020 and the project will be eligible for clean-up costs
  14|                        through MassDevelopment.
  15|                   • In the fall of 2019 the Town Manager worked with a consultant to apply for grant funding
  16|                        through the Municipal Vulnerability Preparedness (MVP) Program, which provides
  17|                        support for cities and towns to plan for climate change and implement key adaptation
  18|                        actions for resiliency. The planning grant applied for is a necessary step in order to apply
  19|                        for construction costs to implement projects that are identified during the planning phase.
  20|                        In 2020, the Town was notified we received this grant.
  21|                   • After successfully applying for District Local Technical Assistance from our regional
  22|                        planning agency to create a business brochure and create a permitting guidebook, both
  23|                        were completed in January. The Business Brochure was created with assistance from the
  24|                        Lunenburg Business Association and Land Use Director in an effort to highlight the
  25|                        town's natural resources and increase awareness of these locations to residents who many
  26|                        not know where public access to trails and conservation lands are located. The Permitting
  27|                        Guidebooks was created to assist customers with projects of any type and scale to simplify
  28|                        how they can file permits. Another project that was applied for and received through our
  29|                        regional planning agency was for a Walkability and Parking Study. This study focused on
  30|                        traffic data, crash data, an inventory of bike and pedestrian infrastructure for the Main
  31|                        Street area and Whalom Lakefront area. The report was finalized in November and
  32|                        identified a number of recommendations to address improving the walkability and parking
  33|                        in both the Main Street and Whalom area.
  34|                                                                         22

===PAGE 25=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                        was held with different stakeholders that would be affected by decisions relative to the
   2|                        future of the TC Passios, old Primary Building, Ritter Memorial, Town Hall, and the
   3|                        Brooks House. On January 15" the Selectmen had a joint meeting with the Architectural
   4|                        Preservation District Commission, Historical Commission, and Historical Society to
   5|                        discuss their concerns regarding the condition of the old Primary School and future of the
   6|                        building. At the conclusion of the meeting, Chairman Jamie Toale stated the goal of the
   7|                        Board was to ask town meeting for funding for detailed plans for the TC Passios so that an
   8|                        actual cost to move town offices there would be known. In February the Board met with
   9|                        the Conservation Commission, Open Space, and the Parks Commission to discuss the plan
  10|                       to use TC Passios for municipal office space, school central offices, and community
  11|                        space. In March the Board met with the Public Access Cable Committee and town
  12|                        employees to hear their input on consolidating town offices at the TC Passios building. In
  13|                        May the Board met with another group of stakeholders including the Council on Aging
  14|                        members, COA Director, Library Trustees, Library Director, and Teen Center personnel.
  15|                        In September the Board met with the Architectural Preservation District Committee to
  16|                        discuss the old Primary building and the Brooks House, in terms of future use and how
  17|                        this aligned with the regulations under the APDC Bylaw. In November the Board
  18|                        conducted a survey of potential uses for the old Primary School that was handed out at the
  19|                        Special Town Meeting and the town meeting also included a non-binding vote on the
  20|                        demolition of the old Primary School. The Board continues to discuss this goal and move
  21|                        forward on a potential re-use.
  22|                    • On January 22nd our legislators, Senator Tran, Representative Benson and Representative
  23|                        Hay came before the board to announce the $130,000 state earmark for abatement of
  24|                        asbestos at the Turkey Hill Elementary School.
  25|                   • In March the Town partnered with the solar company Nexamp to promote their
  26|                        community solar program to Lunenburg residents for discounted electricity. The program
  27|                        was so successful it reached its cap and had a waiting list. Later in the year, Nexamp
  28|                        secured another solar location that enabled those on the waiting list to take advantage of
  29|                        these discounts.
  30|                    • In March the Selectmen created the Land Acquisition Committee to examine and
  31|                        negotiate the purchase of land to be used for conservation and recreation purposes at 994
  32|                        Northfield Road. Ultimately, the Committee was successful in negotiating a price of
  33|                        $775,000, which was below the appraised value and secured $500,000 in grant funds
  34|                        towards the purchase of land to be used for conservation and recreations purposes at 994
  35|                        Northfield Road. Town meeting approved the purchase of land at the November 2019
  36|                        Special Town Meeting.
  37|                                                                          23

===PAGE 26=== (ocr, 35 lines)
   1|                       includea the hiring ot an additional full-lime fatrolman, addressed the need tor increased
   2|                       Overtime costs for the Fire Department, a part-time Recreation Director, and Assistant
   3|                       Town Manager/HR Director. The Annual Town Meeting was held on Saturday, May 4* and
   4|                       approved a total budget for FY20 of $38,058,164.30.
   5|                  • This year was another incredible year for road projects in Lunenburg. Besides the
   6|                       continuation of the $4M Pavement Management Plan on local roads, the Town was the
   7|                       recipient of a Small Bridge Grant that was originally applied for in June of 2017 in the
   8|                       amount of $379,000 to reconstruct the Pleasant Street Bridge over Pearl Hill Brook. The
   9|                       project requires design work phases that must be approved by MassDOT as part of the
  10|                       process. The project is expected to be complete by the spring of 2022. The DPW also
  11|                       undertook culvert repairs on Northfield Road and Sunset Lane and repairs to the Townsend
  12|                       Harbor Bridge. The Summer Street Road Reconstruction project that was awarded in 2018
  13|                       was substantially complete by the fall of 2019.
  14|                       The Town had begun the process of becoming a Dementia Friendly community in the latter
  15|                       half of 2018 and had a kick-off event in June of 2019. The Dementia Friendly Action group
  16|                       had a lot of success in a short amount of time and the initiatives they undertook were
  17|                       modeled by other communities.
  18|                  • As part of the Town Manager's Goals for FY19, the Town Manager completed an
  19|                       Employee Handbook and Procurement Manual. The Employee Handbook codified the
  20|                      town's hiring process, personnel policies and practices and the Procurement Manual
  21|                       provided written procedures for town departments for proper procurement of supplies,
  22|                       services, and construction projects. The Board adopted a Reimbursement Policy.
  23|                       The Selectmen and the Town Manager made their annual appointments in June for FY
  24|                       2020.
  25|                  • In July, as part of an annual review of the Selectmen's Policies and Procedures, the Town
  26|                       Manager presented various updates to the Board's Manual including a revised Recycling
  27|                       Policy, Public Comment Policy, Chapter 61 Policy, among other minor revisions to other
  28|                      policies. The Board also adopted a new Social Media policy.
  29|                  • In October the Town Manager, the School Superintendent, and the Land Use Director held a
  30|                       Symposium to discuss the historical growth patterns and projected future growth/
  31|                      development and how this would impact Town and School services and needs. Some of the
  32|                      patterns that were evident from the data was there would be a steady growth in the gaining
  33|                      population in Lunenburg, a rise in racial and ethnic diversity, the need for increased staffing
  34|                      and the need for additional space for added staff.

   2|                    • The Special Town Meeting in November included a number of articles such as amending
   3|                        the FY 2019 budget to capture additional tax growth, transferred available funds into the
   4|                        Special Purpose Stabilization account, the acquisition of property on Northfield Road for
   5|                        conservation and recreation purposes, by-laws changes, and two Home Rule petitions for
   6|                        additional liquor licenses.
   7|                        In December, the Board approved all the annual liquor licenses, common victualler,
   8|                        weekday entertainment, automatic amusement, jukebox, and motor vehicle licenses.
   9|                        Throughout the year, the Board reviewed Earth Removal permits, liquor license transfers,
  10|                        Special Event permit requests, flammable storage licenses, pole petitions, tollbooth
  11|                        requests, and held Dangerous Dog hearings.
  12|                    As of writing this report, it has been three and a half years since I was appointed your Town
  13|                    Manager and it is a responsibility that I take very seriously. The residents of Lunenburg
  14|                    demonstrate the true meaning of community that I have witnessed in various acts, whether it
  15|                    be supporting our local businesses, non-profit groups raising funds for those in need, or
  16|                    words of gratitude for our public safety and public works departments. I have the privilege
  17|                    of working with many talented individuals that are committed to helping the residents and
  18|                    business owners of Lunenburg and are consistently looking for ways to better serve the
  19|                    public. The accomplishments noted above, among the numerous day-to-day responsibilities,
  20|                    could not happen without the cooperation of dedicated staff, appointed, and elected officials
  21|                    that make the wheels of town government turn. Thank you to my Executive Assistant, Elaine
  22|                    Peterson and Assistant Town Manager Julie Belliveau for all your hard work and keeping the
  23|                    office running smoothly. I would also like to thank the committee and board members,
  24|                    department heads and staff for the high level of service they provide to the residents of town.
  25|                    Specifically, I would like to express gratitude to the department heads that lead our various

   1|                                      Clerk- Cullen Dwyer
   2|                    Member- Brian Corcoran
   3|                   In 2019, the APDC inspected the Old Primary School and pursued ongoing efforts to preserve the structure.
   4|                   Through various meetings and letters, the Town Manager and Select Board were told that the building was in a
   5|                   process of Demolition by Neglect, that it was their responsibility to maintain the building, and that through inaction the town was in violation of the APD bylaw. Outside funding was sought to arrest the deterioration.
   6|                   Negotiations with the Select Board raised the question of how buildings were determined to be in Demolition by
   7|                   Neglect, and if notices of violation were applied evenly. To address the issue, Vice-Chair La Veck drafted a
   8|                   procedure to review the complete inventory of historic buildings in the APD on a semi-annul basis.
   9|                   Neglect of the Brooks House was also a concern. By town meeting vote, the Town Manager was authorized to
  10|                   sell the structure and have it moved to some other location. As this resolution was contrary to the APDC's
  11|                   mission, various town entities were contacted to begin crafting an alternate solution that would preserve the
  12|                   building in its present location.
  13|                    LaVeck had noted a type setting error when the APD bylaw was submitted to the State Attorney General's
  14|                   office for ratification.
  15|                   The error omitted certain portions of the bylaw's text, including the definition of Demolition by Neglect. The
  16|                   Select Board assured the APDC that the error would not affect the application of the bylaw, while the Town
  17|                   Manager endeavored to correct the error.
  18|                   Chairman McGrath led the effort to design and order new road signs to mark the APD, which will be installed
  19|                       he amourg bed the e
  20|                   The APDC reviewed and approved several Certificates to Alter, including:
  21|                   136 Leominster Road- replacement of siding and trim
  22|                   876 Massachusetts Avenue-re-roof of barn
  23|                   92 Leominster Road- re-roof of house
  24|                   909 Massachusetts Avenue (Harley House)- renovation of porch and certain windows and doors.
  25|                   An administrative review was also conducted for Dawn Marshall at 42 Main Street (Captain Jones House) for
  26|                   the construction of an apartment in the barn.

   1|                    Officer. Lisa Normandin serves as the Administrative Assistant to the Building Department,
   2|                   as well as the Zoning Board of Appeals Administrative Assistant. Jack Biery serves as the
   3|                    Wiring Inspector. Gary Williams serves as the Plumbing Inspector and Gas Inspector. The
   4|                    purpose of the Building Department is to ensure State regulations and Codes are being met as
   5|                   well as local bylaws. This includes new construction, alterations, additions, remodels and
   6|                   annual inspections of certain public use groups such as restaurants, churches, bars, daycares,
   7|                   meeting halls, schools, and others.
   8|                   Building Permits for fiscal year 2019 are as follows:
   9|                   120 gas permits
  10|                   122 plumbing permits
  11|                   248 electrical permits
  12|                   461 building permits, including driveway, mechanical, and fire suppression permits
  13|                   951 total permits issued
  14|                   The state of Massachusetts continues to follow a group of International Codes amended by
  15|                   each state. These codes cover a variety of building elements from mechanical systems, energy
  16|                   conservation, primary building practices, installation of fire suppression systems, and others.
  17|                   Currently we are in the 9* edition of 780 CMR, the Massachusetts State Building Code.
  18|                   In addition to the Building Codes and Zoning Bylaws, the Building Department also assists
  19|                   the Town Clerk with Business Certificates. This is achieved by way of a "Form of Intent".
  20|                   This form is used to ensure local bylaws are met by the applicant. Once the form is completed
  21|                   by the Building Department, a Business Certificate can be applied for at the Town Clerk's
  22|                   office.
  23|                   All applications for new work requiring permits are available online through the Town
  24|                   website. The Building Department continues to use ViewPoint online permitting. Paper
  25|                   applications are available at the Building Department office as well.
  26|                   Casey J. Burlingame

   1|                   The mission of the Council on Aging is to advocate for the improved quality of life for all seniors through
   2|                   supportive services, programs and education. Councils on Aging were established under Massachusetts General
   3|                   Law Chapter 40, section 8B, to include setting policy for the administration of elder programs or services and to
   4|                   develop, coordinate and conduct such activities which advocate for elder issues.
   5|                   The FY 19 Council on Aging staff consisted of Susan Doherty, Director; Amanda Koeck, Administrative Assistant
   6|                   and Transportation; Pauline Roy, Outreach Coordinator; Sandra Ricci, Meal Site Coordinator; Judy Alario and
   7|                   Elsa Watson, Meal Site Assistants; Jim McGuigan, Paul Grunditz, and James Barrett, MART Van Drivers.
   8|                   The Council on Aging Board is an eleven member board appointed to 3 year terms by the Lunenburg Board of
   9|                   Selectmen. The FY19 Council on Aging Board members were; Chairperson Pete Lincoln, Vice-Chairperson
  10|                   Jacquelyn Dwyer, Betty DiGiacomo, Sarah Grant, Brian Guenard, Deb Lincoln, Kevin McNally, Cheryl Moisan,
  11|                   Diane Nowd, Jane Rabbitt, and Judith Tarbell.
  12|                   The Town budget alone does not support this senior center. Other sources of funding are the Eagle House
  13|                   Supporters Inc., a non-profit 501c3 group, Formula Grant funding from the Commonwealth of Massachusetts, and
  14|                   Montachusett Regional Transit Authority.
  15|                   We are very lucky to have a large volunteer force at the Eagle House. In FY19 we had 57 volunteers who
  16|                   contributed 2,452.75 hours of their time to help us out by doing things such as driving for the meals on wheels
  17|                  program, program leaders, kitchen helpers, Memory Café, office help, library, and friendly visitors. We greatly
  18|                   appreciate their service.
  19|                   The Eagle House offers a variety of programs, such as health and wellness, arts and crafts, intergenerational,
  20|                   bocce, shuffle board, cards and games, cultural, information and referral, Memory Café and social events both at
  21|                   the senior center and offsite.
  22|                   There were approximately 978 people who utilized the senior center in FY19. Those 978 people compiled 28,764
  23|                  units of duplicated service by participating in the programs listed above.
  24|                  In FY 19 the Council on Aging delivered 4,319 Meals on Wheels to 46 clients and provided 2,964 rides to 71
  25|                   clients on our senior van.
  26|                   We are very fortunate to have a Town Government that supports our center and understands the importance of
  27|                   supporting the Council on Aging to maintain quality services for our elders.
  28|                  Special thanks go to our 501c3 fundraising group, Eagle House Supporters Inc. This group provides funding for
  29|                   new living room furniture, office/kitchen supplies, various programs & entertainment, garden upkeep and spring
  30|                   mulch at the Eagle House Senior Community Center. Also special thanks to all of our outside agencies for
  31|                   donating time and programming to our clients.
  32|                   The Lunenburg Council on Aging newsletter lists current events and is available for you online at
  33|                   www.ourseniorcenter.com or you can pick up a copy at the Eagle House Senior Community Center, Town Hall,
  34|                   Library, Post Office, local churches and various businesses throughout the community.

===PAGE 65=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                      "The mission of the Department of Public Works is to protect, preserve, and improve the lown's
   2|                      infrastructure and related assets in a manner which meets and exceeds the current and future social
   3|                      and economic needs of the community, to contribute to a healthy, safe and quality environment for
   4|                      the Town's residents and visitors, and to provide a cost effective and environmentally sound means
   5|                      of disposing of solid waste and wastewater."
   6|                      DEPARTMENT PHILOSOPHY
   7|                               Honesty and integrity are absolutes. They characterize the only forum in which anything
   8|                      worthwhile can be accomplished.
   9|                               Efficiency: Make every effort to proceed correctly the first time. It may take a bit longer but
  10|                      will not require repeated attempts.
  11|                               Loyalty up and down the chain of command is essential to success.
  12|                               Every employee is essential. Those at the top are support people. The real work is done by
  13|                      the men and women who drive the trucks, operate the equipment, turn the wrenches, and provide a
  14|                      seamless flow of paperwork.
  15|                      DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
  16|                               Public Works staff is either working or on call twenty-four hours a day, three hundred and
  17|                      sixty-five days a year, prepared to respond to any weather, infrastructure, roadway, or facility
  18|                      related emergency that may arise.
  19|                               We strive to enhance the awareness of the value Public Works provides to our Town, and
  20|                      ensure others are aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to
  21|                      make sure efforts are not dismissed as they merge into the fabric of our everyday expectation of
  22|                      service.
  23|                               Major events, particularly in the form of devastating storms, over the past years have
  24|                      highlighted the fact that Public Works has a seat at the table and is a major player in emergency
  25|                      management as first responders. Town Officials, Police, and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is
  26|                      critical in order to facilitate the required services of others. We must continue to build on this
  27|                      awareness.
  28|                               This department must reach out to the public we serve, continuing to make them aware of
  29|                      the fact that the common services they receive do not just happen, but rather are the efforts of a
  30|                      dedicated staff of individuals, service companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater control,
  31|                      solid waste collection and recycling operations, tree removal, storm water management, grounds
  32|                      maintenance, winter snow and ice removal, cemeteries and parks are necessary services supporting
  33|                       everyone in the community.
  34|                               We will continue to engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather
  35|                      support for annual budget requests, capital requests, and specific projects.
  36|                                                                          63

  10|                   PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  11|                             It had become a high priority goal of the Board of Selectmen as Road Commissioners, to
  12|                   achieve a pavement condition of 72%-75% town-wide. While state-supported Chapter 90 funds remain
  13|                    tagnant. the dedicated Pavement Management line in the operational budget has grown substantially
  14|                     additionally, with the infusion of funds facilitated by a four million dollar debt exclusion supported br
  15|                   residents, the Comprehensive Pavement Management Plan has been hugely successful providing
  16|                   smooth and safe transportation for our residents and all those travelling through our community.
  17|                            The debt exclusion funding combined with state and operational funds has nearly tripled the
  18|                   total paving value of years past as we attack a nine million dollar backlog of repairs.
  19|                            Time spent addressing potholes and other roadway deficiencies diminish exponentially as new
  20|                   paving increases. A clear benefit of the dollars expended. There is much work yet to do but expectations
  21|                   are high.
  22|                            The Summer Street project burst into full development after many years of aggressive lobbying
  23|                   by this department, Town otticials and members of our legislative branch. As of this writing, the ten
  24|                   million dollar rehabilitation of sub-surface utilities and total reconstruction of the roadway from
  25|                   Leominster to John Fitch Highway in Fitchburg is complete. This represents the single largest roadway
  26|                   project in the history of the Town of Lunenburg
  27|                            In addition, funding has been procured for the reconstruction of the Pleasant St. Bridge through
  28|                   the State's Small Bridge program. We have also been awarded a grant to enhance the "walkability" in
  29|                   town center by way of a successful application to the Complete Streets Design Committee and the
  30|                   Office of the Secretary of Transportation.
  31|                             Acquiring supplemental funding allows the completion of projects that would otherwise not be
  32|                   viable due to the extraordinary expense.

  35|                   vehicles and equipment. High performance police units, first responder Fire and Rescue apparatus, park,
  36|                   cemetery and school vehicles as well as over 30 pieces of highway-related machinery
  37|                                                                         64

   1|                    As Director, I have developed and submitted to Town Officials a vehicle replacement schedule based
   2|                   upon that created by the American Public Works Association in order to more clearly represent our
   3|                   capital needs.
   4|                   FACILITIES MANAGEMENT
   5|                   The following is a list of buildings and properties maintained by the DPW:
   6|                                 DPW Facility 520 Chase Road                     Public Safety Complex
   7|                                 Park Bldg. Leominster Rd.                       Town Hall
   8|                                 Town Beach                                      Town Common
   9|                                 Ritter Bldg.                                    Teen Center

  11|                                 The Eagle House
  12|                                 All athletic fields/playgrounds excluding the school campus.
  13|                    A facilities staff of one individual remains on-call 24 hours a day 365 days per year.
  14|                    Collectively we attempt to keep buildings compliant with current codes to insure a safe, secure work
  15|                    environment. Any significant interruption of service within Town buildings carries the potential of
  16|                   disrupting or disabling necessary services provided to the community. The Facilities Director is a
  17|                    problem solver and technician with a wide range of experience encompassing all of the construction
  18|                    trades. Together with a stable of reliable professionals, we deliver services directly or oversee those
  19|                    functions provided by specialized subcontractors. Unfortunately, the creation of a capital replacement
  20|                    program and a definitive plan for all our buildings has fallen short of expectations. Years of neglect
  21|                    have created a significant backlog of necessary repairs and improvements and the process has become
  22|                    reactionary at best. The Building Reuse Committee reported clearly that asset management relative to
  23|                    our buildings requires a professionally prepared assessment and a long-range capital plan. To this end,
  24|                    our plan for 2021 is to continue with the contracted services of a Project Management Consultant to
  25|                    survey, quantify, project building needs and establish an annual cost. Additionally, we will revisit the
  26|                    Vertex Report and identify "envelope" tasks that if funded, would protect the structures from further
  27|                    exterior failure. Public Works continues its use of a building maintenance software program as a
  28|                    framework for day-to-day activities. Requests for service are acknowledged immediately, prioritized,
  29|                    and completed in a timely manner either in-house or by sub-contracted services. Those individuals
  30|                    requesting service are notified upon completion of the tasks and a history of the repair is filed
  31|                    electronically.
  32|                    Until we as a town can achieve consensus in the form of a Town-wide plan, we will make little
  33|                    progress. For example, years have passed and still there is no clear path as to the future of the old
  34|                    Primary School. As caretakers it is difficult to create a definitive approach given the uncertainty of future use.
  35|                                                                          65

===PAGE 68=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                    to avoid paving over problems. In the future new Federal and State storm water regulations will likely
   2|                    force the Town to become more proactive albeit with no additional identified source of funding.
   3|                              At the conclusion of winter and again in the late fall, drainage structures in close proximity to
   4|                    open water are cleaned and inspected. Public Works maintains an aggressive schedule of street
   5|                    sweeping and catch basin cleaning, the front line of storm water management.
   6|                    Waste Water
   7|                             The DP W continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system. Day to day maintenance of the
   8|                    overall wastewater system inclusive of 10 pump stations is outsourced to a specialty vendor but
   9|                    continues to be the responsibility of Public Works. Business management, inspections, and permitting,
  10|                    remain in-house and the responsibility of the DPW.
  11|                    Tree Removal
  12|                             Storm damage together with the natural attrition creates a backlog of tree felling and pruning
  13|                    that far exceeds the allotted budget. The Department is unable to fulfill each resident request and must
  14|                    prioritize work based upon the calculated degree of danger to residents and property. With the
  15|                    establishment of a Hazardous Tree Survey, a more efficient approach to defects has been established
  16|                    through prioritization. Sadly, the amount of work far exceeds the available revenue. Until there is a
  17|                    substantial infusion of funds, the DPW will act in a reactionary mode. All work aside from brush
  18|                    removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees is outsourced due to the necessity of a bucket
  19|                    truck or crane and a licensed professional. Each spring DPW staff initiates the activity of removing and
  20|                    disposing of woody debris within the right-of-way together with a town-wide program of curbside
  21|                   mowing. An aggressive program by our energy provider in recent years has lessened the burden of the
  22|                    Town to remove those trees that may cause an interruption of electrical service to the community.
  23|                    Landfill
  24|                           The "capped" landfill off Young's Road remains in compliance with the inspection protocol
  25|                    mandated by the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored regularly for excessive
  26|                    methane gas as well as groundwater contamination.
  27|                             The frequency of the testing, reduced through a special permit as a result of consistently
  28|                    favorable results, remained constant throughout the year. The landfill site including the perimeter
  29|                    fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
  30|                    determined by the EPA to be hazardous waste. We are allowed to store these materials through the
  31|                    procurement ot a (BUD) Beneticial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires yearly testing of
  32|                    these materials. Other communities must transport these tainted materials to an appropriate site at a
  33|                    substantial cost to the community.
  34|                             On weekends during the fall and spring, DPW personnel oversee and manage the annual
  35|                    composting of brush, leaves and other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and
  36|                    our overall recycling program.

   1|                    trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside collection of trash and recyclables by our current vendor,
   2|                    Casella Inc. Pay as you throw ... Recently we entered into an agreement allowing our "trash bag"
   3|                    supplier to coordinate the effort between supplier and vendor resulting in some mixed reviews. DPW
   4|                    remains the point of contact between residents and the vendor responsible for curbside pick-up.
   5|                    Lake Shirley Dam
   6|                    In the early 1990s with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding, the Lake Shirley Association turned
   7|                    over ownership of the dam to the Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for
   8|                    maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed by a small number of volunteers from the
   9|                    Lake Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam regulations, the
  10|                    establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was placed under the
  11|                    umbrella of the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP
  12|                    mandates compliance with current and future regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and
  13|                    support of a professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection
  14|                    resulted in a positive report and we remain in total compliance.
  15|                    Winter Operations
  16|                    Snow and ice present a major challenge. The safety of the public depends on the ability of the DPW to
  17|                    maintain our roadways at a high level of safety despite the severity or the duration of snow and icing
  18|                    events. To that end, we have developed a winter operations procedure based upon a detailed plan with a
  19|                    flexibility to accommodate the unpredictability of each storm. The expectation of immediate service is
  20|                    high and must be delivered to every part of town in a timely manner.
  21|                             Departmental preparedness begins with successful implementation of the capital plan.
  22|                     Financial support is necessary to provide effective fleet rotation insuring minimal interruption of
  23|                    services due to breakdowns and repairs. In the fall, salt is delivered to the Chase Rd. facility.
  24|                     Lunenburg is in a consortium with 14 other communities in an effort to procure the lowest possible
  25|                    price. From late summer to early fall vehicle maintenance staff inspects and prepares the trucks,
  26|                    spreaders and plows for winter operations. During the construction season road crews address right-of-
  27|                    way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects in order to eliminate obstacles to snow
  28|                    removal. Each division within the DPW is involved in winter operations. Typically, DPW staff
  29|                    combines with up to 14 subcontractors to provide service.
  30|                             Generally, it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event, that determines the
  31|                     overall cost. For example, a fast moving snow storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less than a
  32|                    long duration freezing rain event even though the accumulation is much less. Historically, the department operates in a deficit due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost of an average winter.
  33|                             It is a dedicated group of individuals who successfully challenge the worst Nature has to offer
  34|                     regardless of severity or duration.
  35|                                               "DPW workers are the original Storm Troopers"
  36|                                                                          67

   1|                   changes in population and funding. In November, the lown Manager provided a target budget to
   2|                   department heads as a guide to develop their budget requests. After individual discussions with the
   3|                   department heads and listening to public comment during the budget presentations, the Town Manager
   4|                   developed a final recommended budget. Hard questions were thoughtfully talked through in pursuit of
   5|                   finding balance between ideal service and what can be funded at projected levels of taxes and new
   6|                   growth. The fiscal year 2020 budget recommendation is for a 3% increase over FY19. The budget
   7|                   would fund targeted additions in staff in response to the increased need for Town services resulting from
   8|                   increased population. It funds the capital plan and the Town's stabilization accounts. It meets all the
   9|                   financial policy requirements as set forth per Town Policy. The budget does not call for increases in
  10|                   debt; the Town would continue to pay down its debt through FY 2020 and beyond. We are pleased to
  11|                   report for the upcoming year Lunenburg remains fiscally healthy.
  12|                         Revenue
  13|                         Lunenburg's total revenues are projected to be $43,133,920. The projection brings in an
  14|                         additional $1,508,020 above what was collected last year. The estimated revenue breakdown
  15|                        for FY20 is:
  16|                                 Source                  Amount       % of Total
  17|                     Tax Levy            $27,953,184       65%
  18|                    State Aid            $ 9,250,358       21%
  19|                    Local Receipts       $3,010,153         7%
  20|                     Available Fund      $2,920,225         7%
  21|                   The tax levy is projected to increase by $904,568: the standard 2.5% increase over last year's tax base,
  22|                   projected new growth of $375,000, and excludable debt. State Aid is projected to increase by 1.9%,
  23|                   although it still has not reached the robust levels seen in past years. The Town benefits from non-
  24|                   municipal revenues (e.g. grants, support groups, and donations). For example, the Library, Council on
  25|                   Aging, and Fire Department have received funding for operating and capital expenses in the past year
  26|                   from sources other than local taxes and state aid, thus keeping those expenses out of the budget. We are
  27|                   grateful for the efforts that all departments put into finding outside sources of funding to help the Town
  28|                   continue to move in a positive direction.
  29|                   Expenses
  30|                   Lunenburg's recommended expenditures are $43,133,920. Out of this total the Omnibus Budget total is
  31|                   $38,058,164, which is an increase of $1,190,260 or a 3.2% increase. Other expenditure categories
  32|                   include Cherry Sheet Charges ($1,215,303), Worcester Regional Retirement ($1,354,353), capital
  33|                   articles ($1,684,100), tax title ($12,000), allowance for abatements ($175,000), funding for stabilization
  34|                   accounts and OPEB ($450,000), and other non-operating expenses ($185,000). The total overall budget
  35|                   increases $1,508,020, or a 3.6% increase.

  38|                    the Other Post-Employment Benefits (OPEB) fund ($150,000.

  41|                    Town's continuing AA+ bond rating. However, a recent Debt and Liability assessment found that

  43|                    the Fiscal 2020 budget and will be an important factor when looking ahead.

   5|                     The 18th century Battles House at 68 Elm Street was slated for demolition to make way for the
   6|                     development of new homes. The Historical Commission invoked the demolition delay bylaw to seek
   7|                     alternatives. By some combination of pressure from the Commission, cooperation of the property

  19|                    Lunenburg continues to own historical buildings in the town center. The old Primary School and to a

   1|                    giving more." I see evidence of that every day in my colleagues at the library. It is a joy for all of us to
   2|                    work at the library and to see the burgeoning activity here: more than 80,000 items borrowed this past
   3|                    year, almost 70,000 visitors, and more than 8,000 people attending more than 400 programs this past
   4|                    year. Most afternoons you'll find dozens of people doing homework, playing board or video games,
   5|                    researching, using the computers, and even hanging out at the library!
   6|                    I hope you're not surprised that the library is about more than just books. Residents come to the library
   7|                    just to see a smiling face; community members and groups have meetings at the library; families come
   8|                    to story-time; a father and son eat dinner weekly in our cafe for time out of the house; teens and adults
   9|                    volunteer and gain job-like experience; jobseekers come for help on their job search; entire classes of
  10|                    fifth graders come to learn to use the library; people come for help answering a question, to borrow our
  11|                    telescope or electricity use monitor or ukulele; they use ebooks or audiobooks from home or their car;
  12|                     they attend an exercise program or listen to a lecture in the community room; some even watch library
  13|                     programs on the local access cable channel; and of course, many people come to see a movie or
  14|                     borrow and discuss a book.
  15|                    There are new faces on staff at your library. Retirements and graduations mean that five people
  16|                    working at the library are new to their jobs in 2019-we only employ ten total-and the staff is happier
  17|                    than ever to serve our community. The infusion of youth in our staff combines perfectly with several
  18|                    experienced, long-tenured workers with vast amounts of institutional knowledge and dedication. As we
  19|                    move forward into this new era, we are focusing in particular on collaboration and communication. We
  20|                    collaborate amongst ourselves as a staff, we collaborate with other community organizations, and we
  21|                    collaborate with other town departments, all to better serve the community. We also recognize that one
  22|                     of our biggest opportunities is to better communicate to our community all of the services we offer.
  23|                     When someone in town has a challenge, we want them to first ask themselves, "Can the library help
  24|                     me with that?"
  25|                     Our facilities also continue to be renewed. Our building is now more than 15 years old, a fact that
  26|                     surprises many people who enter it for the first time, and to retain that like-new character we must
  27|                     continue to renew it. We have to thank the generosity of the Friends of Lunenburg Public Library for
  28|                     their role in keeping the library in its gem-like condition. They contribute more than $30,000 each year
  29|                     to library goals, including programming, maintenance, collections, and supplies. Without the support
  30|                     of the Friends, the library would be in a much more challenging position.
  31|                     Growing from the successful 2018 renovation of the teen room—one of the key contributing factors
  32|                     that has led to an explosion of teens spending their weekday afternoons in the library-we've focused
  33|                     on creating a space for "tweens" as well. Coupled with a partnership with Turkey Hill Elementary
  34|                     School to bring fifth grade students to the library to learn to use our resources, we've seen an
  35|                     expanding number of library users from the 10-12 year old demographic this past year, too, especially
  36|                     in response to programs targeted specifically at this group.

   1|                   to continue to expand the number and variety of programs we offer our adult community. Between

  17|                   Children's Services
  18|                   Our community members have already discovered that the Lunenburg Public Library, in addition to
  19|                   being a valued part of our community, is an integral part of early childhood development. Attendance
  20|                   at weekly story times for children ages birth-5 remains strong. Our afterschool offerings for school-
  21|                   aged children include LPL Kids Book Club, which occurs monthly and the children are enjoying the
  22|                   lively book discussions, and related snacks. Enrollment in this group has increased 50% over last year!
  23|                   We also offer monthly Lego Club Challenges that are well attended by both girls and boys and
  24|                   encourages child/parent interaction and offers a STEM component.
  25|                   Similarly, our monthly science, technology, engineering, art and math (STEAM) program also targets
  26|                   elementary aged children to encourage and excite these young learners. A strong knowledge base in
  27|                   these core subjects is one of the keys to a successful future, and this focus echoes school curriculum.
  28|                   Our community outreach and support continue to benefit many area schools and groups. In addition to
  29|                   visits from the fifth graders of Turkey Hill Elementary School, we provide weekly story times for an
  30|                   active group of toddlers and families who are part of School Linked Services, preschoolers from the
  31|                   Early Learning Center and various local family day care groups.
  32|                   Thanks to a gift from the endowment of the Friends of Lunenburg Public Library, we now have two
  33|                   early literacy computers with touch screen technology. Featuring games, storytelling programs, and
  34|                   other resources, they are an excellent resource for early learners to develop pre-reading and literacy
  35|                   skills.

  17|                   Learning (SEL) objectives in mind. We found success with these programs, and will apply this
  18|                  framework to our future teen programming to improve its effectiveness.

  20|                   library partners with the 4H Worcester Extension Office to provide a babysitting training class for
  21|                   tweens ages 11 and up, and in September, the library responded to a community need with a Babysitting
  22|                   Connection Program so that parents could meet with certified teen sitters to find the best match.

  24|                   opportunity to earn community service hours, engaging in supportive activities, like creating cards for
  25|                   the Pink Revolution Breast Cancer Alliance Initiative and stitching for a cause with the library's
  26|                   Stitchcraft group.

  28|                   campaigns run by different groups. Sharing leadership responsibilities and rotating Dungeon Masters

   1|                    2019 Committee Members:
   2|                     Robert Hamel, Chairperson; Susan Doherty, Vice-Chairperson; Sheila Craigen, Clerk; Nancy Forest,
   3|                    Myleen Mallari
   4|                    The Lunenburg Property Tax Work-Off Program offers income eligible senior citizens age 60† an
   5|                    opportunity to have an abatement applied to their property tax bill. Participants worked in various
   6|                    town departments to earn the tax abatement doing general office, custodial, library shelving, Meals
   7|                    on Wheels, and kitchen assistance duties.
   8|                     From January 1, 2019 to June 30, 2019 participants received $10 for each hour worked to earn the
   9|                    $1,000 abatement. At this time income eligibility for participants was $40,000 for a one-person
  10|                    household and $60,000 for a two-person household.
  11|                     The Lunenburg Property Tax Work-Off committee voted at the March 27, 2019 meeting to increase
  12|                    income eligibility for participants of this program to $50,000 for one person households and $75,000
  13|                    for two-person households.
  14|                     Also voted at that meeting was an increase in the hourly wage from $10 to $12 per hour along with
  15|                     an increase to the abatement cap up to $1,500. These increases became effective at the beginning of
  16|                    FY20 (July 1, 2019).
  17|                     There were seven Lunenburg residents who participated from January 1, 2019 - November 1, 2019.
  18|                     These individuals as a whole, accumulated 837.25 hours which amounted to $8,761.00 in abatements.
  19|                    Eligibility Requirements:
  20|                     * Applicants must be 60 years of age or older
  21|                     *Own and occupy a home in Lunenburg as their primary residence for at least one year
  22|                     *Provide proof of income by submitting prior year income tax return information
  23|                     *Receive a property tax bill in their name or their spouse's name who is also 60 years of age or older
  24|                     *Meet the income guidelines of: one person household - $50,000 / two person household - $75,000
  25|                    *All new applicants are required to have a CORI background check and must re-apply and be
  26|                     accepted annually for participation in the program.
  27|                    Earnings are exempt from state taxes only. Earnings are subject to federal and FICA tax
  28|                    withholdings and are also subject to OBRA if participants are not receiving a pension from either
  29|                     Worcester Regional Retirement or Massachusetts State Teachers Retirement.
  30|                    Departments that participated between January 1, 2019 - November 1, 2019
  31|                     *Lunenburg Board of Health
  32|                     *Lunenburg Building Department/Zoning
  33|                     * Lunenburg Council on Aging
  34|                     * Lunenburg DPW/Sewer Departments

===PAGE 86=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                     Joe for his service and leadership as Chairman.
   2|                      The Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Sewer Enterprise Fund, planning
   3|                     for the future and developing and implementing rules, regulations and policies. Under the Town's
   4|                      Charter, responsibility for the day-to-day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are
   5|                     under the supervision of the Town Manager who delegates the responsibility to manage and maintain
   6|                     the operational side of the system to the Department of Public Works Director Jack Rodriquenz. The
   7|                      Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 78% of Lunenburg
   8|                     sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster, and the other 22% is discharged to the City of
   9|                     Fitchburg.
  10|                     The Sewer Commission has contracted with Small Water Systems Services (SWSS) for the
  11|                     operations and management of the ten pumping stations,
  12|                     infrastructure since 2013. In F19, we completed the 6 * year with Sine as our contractor for the
  13|                     supervision of the town's sewer system. The Commission developed a scope of services for a new
  14|                     O&M contract and put it out for bid in April 2019. Small Water Services (SWSS) won the bid and
  15|                     the Commission began negotiations with SWSS for the new contract effective 7/1/19.
  16|                     There are currently 879 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 352 which have
  17|                     access to Town Sewer but are not yet connected. During FY19, 24 new accounts connected to Town
  18|                     Sewer. The Commission billed out $835,329.23 in quarterly sewer bills, and had $44,556 of prior
  19|                     year delinquencies added to the owners' property tax bills for non-payment. Five properties were
  20|                     assessed a privilege fee in FY19. Two properties assessed were residential. Two assessments were
  21|                     for 24-unit apartment buildings and one other property was a 3-unit condominium building.
  22|                     Privilege fees help to pay down the debt owed by the Town on the original sewer project.
  23|                     Throughout FY19, the Commission continued to work with several different groups regarding their
  24|                     interest in sewer extensions, specifically at Lancaster Avenue, Upland Avenue and Chase Road. The
  25|                     Commission is always willing to discuss a potential sewer extension.
  26|                     In FY19, the Commission worked on updates to the Grease Trap Regulations, Connection Charges
  27|                     Policy, Irrigation Meter Policy and Single Meter Policy. On October 30 and November 27, 2018,
  28|                     the Sewer Commission held public hearings to inform residents of the proposed changes. The
  29|                     Commission voted to accept the updated policies on November 27, 2019. For the new Irrigation
  30|                     Meter policy, the Commission investigated options for new updated wireless irrigation meters and
  31|                     worked with the Lunenburg Water District to select the new meter which can be read remotely by
  32|                     the Water District on their regular rounds. With the updated Irrigation Meter policy that was
  33|                     effective on August 1, 2019, irrigation meter customers will be charged an annual fee of $50 for
  34|                     the cost to administer the program. The new policy will require residents with deduct meters to
  35|                     convert their unit to one compatible with the Neptune remote reader by July of 2020.
  36|                     The Sewer Commission developed a scope of work for a maintenance plan for the sewer system.
  37|                     The contract was awarded to Wright Pierce and began in FY19. Wright Pierce will conduct an
  38|                     evaluation of the equipment at all pump stations, working with Small Water Systems to develop a
  39|                     written Maintenance Plan to insure the smooth operation of the system. The final plan will be
  40|                     presented in FY20.
  41|                                                                         84

===PAGE 87=== (ocr, 39 lines)
   1|                   worked with multiple establishments in correcting their non-compliance. The "Rules & Regulations for
   2|                   Oil & Grease Traps/Interceptors for Food Establishments" continues to be a focus of the Commission as
   3|                   Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance costs of the
   4|                   sewer system. The Commission developed a FOG Audit inspection checklist and made plans to begin
   5|                   audits of food establishments. While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on commercial and public
   6|                   disposal, individual homeowners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system
   7|                   which increases everyone's costs. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain.
   8|                   In March 2019, the Commission voted to authorize a contract with Northern Data for bill print
   9|                   outsourcing to streamline the billing process, which has continually grown as more customers have been
  10|                   added to the sewer system. The Commission also voted to join the town's Solar Credit Program in FY19
  11|                   and anticipates saving on energy costs for running the pump stations in FY20.
  12|                   At the May 2019 Town Meeting, the Commission put forward three articles which related to the FY20
  13|                   budget and stabilization funds. For the standard budget article for FY20, $675,427.86 was appropriated
  14|                   from Sewer Enterprise revenues and the sum of $163,251.14 was appropriated from Retained Earnings
  15|                   for a total appropriation of $ 838,679.00 to operate the Sewer Enterprise Fund for FY20. The amount of
  16|                   $888,706.40 was approved to cover both the Enterprise Fund's share of Town services and debt service
  17|                   on the sewer betterments or the total approved cost of $1,727,385.40. The second article transferred
  18|                   monies to the Capital Reserve Stabilization Fund in the amount of $27,521, approximately 3% of
  19|                   proposed revenues as the Commission had done for FY18. This fund is to be used to maintain and
  20|                   improve the operating efficiency of the system or cover large emergency expenditures. A main goal of
  21|                   the Sewer Commission continues to be maintaining a balanced budget every year without having to
  22|                   raise rates for our customers. This is the sixth year that the operating budget has been balanced without
  23|                   a rate increase to the users. The third warrant article was to transfer the sum of $656.00 into the OPEB
  24|                   Sewer Trust Fund. All three articles passed at Town Meeting. It was determined in FY 19 that any
  25|                   monies collected for the Sewer Bank (I/I fees) or Reserve Capacity Stabilization Funds would be
  26|                   transferred at the Special Town Meeting following the close of the fiscal year. A portion of the
  27|                   connection charges collected at the time of an application for a permit to connect goes into these
  28|                   accounts and targets resolving I/I issues and future capacity costs paid to the cities of Fitchburg and
  29|                   Leominster for increased capacities.
  30|                   The Commission continued working in FY19 with Adam Costa (Town Counsel of Mead, Talerman
  31|                   and Costa for guidance in contract negotiations and clarification of regulations. The Commission would
  32|                   also like to thank Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Director, for the seamless and smooth operation of the Sewer
  33|                   Division of the DPW. They would also like to acknowledge and thank the Lunenburg Water District for
  34|                   their partnership and good working relationship with the Sewer Division in providing valuable
  35|                   assistance with meter readings, ownership changes, and status updates.
