   1|                     The Board of Health oversees the Septic System Repair Program which helps low to moderate income
   2|                     families replace failed septic systems or connect to public sewer. Anyone interested in finding out
   3|                     more about this program can contact the Board of Health office.
   4|                     Homeland Security and Emergency Preparedness planning is ongoing. Meetings continue between
   5|                     our agents, other Town departments and regional entities to develop a plan for not only Lunenburg
   6|                     but with our partner towns in our region.
   7|                     This year we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new
   8|                     wells, repairs or upgrades to septic systems and well permits. The Board continues its vigilance with
   9|                     regard to septic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and nuisance complaints and its
  10|                     general mandate to protect the environment and groundwater of the Commonwealth.
  11|                     The Board of Health provides other numerous services in cooperation with our Health Agents, the
  12|                     Nashoba Associated Boards of Health. In addition to our environmental services, numerous clinics are
  13|                     provided throughout the year to our residents. These clinics provide inoculations, including flu shots,
  14|                     blood pressure screenings, Well Oldster Clinics and dental screening. Fluoride treatment and dental
  15|                     checks of our youngsters in the School System are provided by the Nashoba dental staff.
  16|                     The Town of Lunenburg participates in the Central Mass. Mosquito Control Project. Mosquito
  17|                     Control monitors the mosquito population in Lunenburg and advises of any potential diseases such as
  18|                     EEE and West Nile Virus and sprays frequently to control the population during the warmer times of
  19|                     the year. Residents who want to have their property sprayed can do so by emailing to:
  20|                     cттcp@cmmcp.org, calling (508) 393-3055, completing a request on their webpage at: http://
  21|                     www.cmmcp.org, or calling the Board of Health office. This service is free of charge to residents.
  22|                     With our Nashoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in home visits to residents recovering
  23|                     from illness and hospice care and counseling to those in need of this service.
  24|                     Please contact our office in the Ritter Building for more information.
  25|                     Please remember that our board members volunteer their time and efforts on behalf of you, the
  26|                     citizens of Lunenburg.

===PAGE 96=== (ocr, 36 lines)
   1|                                                              FIRE DEPARTMENT
   2|                        The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are
   3|                     dedicated to providing the highest quality, most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to
   4|                                                       the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
   5|                             In 2019 your Fire Department responded to 1885 calls for service, broken down as follows:
   6|                              Fires & Hazardous Conditions             420                 Emergency Medical     949
   7|                             Inspections                                554                Other                     26
   8|                              Current Fire Department staffing is now 7 Career and 40-45 On Call Firefighters and
   9|                     Emergency Medical Technicians, plus 1 full-time Administrative Assistant/EMS Coordinator. The
  10|                     station is manned 7 days per week with 2 Firefighter/Emergency Medical Technicians, plus a 35d
  11|                     Firefighter/EMT Monday-Friday from 8 am-4 pm.               This coverage is a combination of full-time
  12|                     personnel in conjunction with 88 hours per week of per diem coverage. The per diem coverage is
  13|                     provided by our Call Firefighter/EMTs staffing the station at their hourly rate. Staffing the station with
  14|                     dual trained Fire/EMS personnel ensures that we are ready to respond to whatever emergency occurs.
  15|                             Though we have achieved 24 hour coverage in the station, many EMS calls and nearly all fire
  16|                     calls still require more personnel than are on duty in the station. This means we still rely heavily on
  17|                    the dedication and commitment of our Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians. Our call
  18|                     personnel remain and will remain for the foreseeable future, a vital part of our emergency response. A
  19|                     new Call Firefighter/EMT must attend over 340 hours of initial training plus additional time in
  20|                    orientation and field training. Members qualifying to drive fire apparatus must complete an additional
  21|                     65 hours of classroom and practical training. On average, it takes a new member one and a half to two
  22|                    years to fully complete this training.
  23|                             The department continues to move towards the goal of providing our own Paramedic Level
  24|                     Ambulance Service. Though we currently operate two ambulances at the Advanced EMT Level, we
  25|                    must still rely on outside agencies to provide paramedic level care when needed. Firefighter Timothy
  26|                    Downey completed his paramedic training at the end of 2019 and is currently going through the state
  27|                    testing process. Firefighter Austin Flagg also began the training in September and will be completing
  28|                    it at the end of 2020. These two members, plus addition of three more firefighter/paramedics over the
  29|                     next 2-3 years will allow us to provide our own paramedic level care to the community.
  30|                             In 2019, the Fire Prevention Division conducted 554 Inspections.             This is in addition to
  31|                                                                               By law we are required to inspect smoke and
  32|                     carbon monoxide detector installations when a home is sold. We are also required to inspect all new
  33|                     o1l burner and propane gas installations. Any business holding a liquor license is also required to be
  34|                    inspected for fire safety issues. In addition, all new construction projects must have their plans
  35|                    reviewed for proper fire protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction process.
  36|                                                                         94

  22|                    Horton Ambulance was received in late 2019 and is now in service as our primary ambulance. Rescue
  23|                    1 has been rotated to Backup/2nd Ambulance. The replacement for our 31-year-old ladder truck
  24|                   experienced a delay in construction and is now due to arrive in April of 2020. This unit is expected to
  25|                    serve the town for 25+ years. The replacement for Engine 4 has been ordered and is due to arrive in the
  26|                    summer of 2020. This unit is expected to serve the town for 20+ years. The next anticipated major
  27|                    apparatus purchases are not until 2026 (Rescue 1) and 2028 (Squad 3)
  28|                            In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their
  29|                    outstanding commitment to the Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to
  30|                    impress me every day. I would also like to thank the other department heads, boards and commissions
  31|                    for their continuing help. Most people do not realize the level of interdepartmental cooperation that
  32|                    occurs in the daily course of town business. My thanks also go out to the citizens of Lunenburg for
  33|                   their ongoing support of public safety. It is that support that allows us to continue providing the high
  34|                    level of service you have come to expect from us.

   7|                                              Reserve Officer Stan Barney (Lieutenant Retired)
   8|                     Stan Barney began his distinguished career in Law Enforcement as a Reserve officer with the
   9|                     Lunenburg Police Department in 1975. In 1977 Stan was appointed as a full time officer and attended
  10|                     the Massachusetts State Police Academy. In 1983 Stan left to take a job with the Massachusetts
  11|                     Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals, (MSPCA), as an enforcement officer. During that
  12|                     time Stan stayed on as a Reserve officer and returned as a full time officer in 1996. He was later
  13|                     promoted to Sergeant and then Acting Lieutenant. Stan retired in 2013 but also elected to stay on as a
  14|                     Reserve officer to help with the staffing shortage. In 2019 Stan decided to retire completely giving up
  15|                     his commission as a Reserve officer. We were grateful that Stan stayed with us as long as he did and
  16|                     we have always been extremely honored to serve with Stan. We thank Lieutenant Stan Barney for all
  17|                     his years of exceptional service and wish him the best of luck and the most enjoyment with his
  18|                     retirement.
  19|                                                          About the Police Department
  20|                     The Police Department is in operation 24 hours a day, 7 days a week. The department is staffed with
  21|                     the Chief of Police supported by the Public Safety Coordinator, and one Administrative Lieutenant.
  22|                     Three Sergeants/Shift commanders, one of the sergeants presently assigned to K-9/Traffic
  23|                     enforcement, eight Patrol Officers, three Student Officers, and eight Reserve Intermittent Patrol
  24|                     by a Detective Sergeant. Within the C.I.U. is the School Resource Officer who is also assigned to Utficers make up the patrol unit. Supporting the patrol unit is the Criminal Investigation Unit headed
  25|                     Community Outreach and who is accompanied by Hank the therapy dog. The Lieutenant and Public
  26|                     Safety Coordinator are on duty Monday through Friday between the hours of 7AM and 3PM. Office
  27|                     hours for the Chief of Police are 8AM to 4PM Monday through Friday.
  28|                     The Animal Control Officer responds to domestic animal calls on a 24 hour, 7 day per week basis.
  29|                     This year 356 Animal calls were logged. The public is reminded to report any animal related
  30|                     complaints to the Regional Call Center at (978) 582-4531. For emergencies, dial 911. More
  31|                     information can be found on the Animal Control Web site at lunenburgma.gov.
  32|                     Dogs must be licensed by April 1" annually. Dogs must have a current Rabies certificate to qualify
  33|                     for licensing. This can be done at the Clerk's office in Town Hall, 17 Main Street Lunenburg, MA
  34|                     01462. Unspayed Female/Unaltered Male $20 fee. Spayed Female/Altered Male $5 fee. Late Fee:
  35|                     The front desk is open Monday thru Friday from 7AM to 7PM. Emergency, Non-emergency and
  36|                     911 calls are routed through Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District, (NVRDD).
  37|                              For all emergencies, dial 911. For general calls, please call (978) 582-4531.
  38|                     Applications for Firearms Licensing can be found on the Police Department Website at
  39|                                        lunenburgma.gov. Contact (978) 582-4531 for an appointment.

===PAGE 102=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                   kills.
   2|                   district for emotional support and many other initiatives such as,   "read with a dog." Hank was also
   3|                   deployed to a number of funerals for emotional support as well, and to the Domestic Violence Round
   4|                   table, Cub Scout visits and the Senior Center just to name a few. In 2019, staff assignments had to be
   5|                   increased 197 times to effectively meet service demands.
   6|                   The day shift desk officer answered 3056 calls and took 4666 lobby inquiries and complaints in 2019.
   7|                   The day shift desk officer wrote 82 incident reports in 2019. This year the day shift desk watch officer
   8|                   was responsible for conducting suicide watch and assisting with the court processing of 83 detainees;
   9|                   either taken into custody overnight or during the day shift. The day shift desk officer processed 322
  10|                   Firearms Licenses, a 109% increase over the previous year, 12 Non FID related fingerprints, and
  11|                   registered 15 sex offenders. The 3 pm to 7 pm desk watch officers assisted in the processing of 55
  12|                   detainees while providing suicide watch and numerous other services including providing support for the
  13|                   patrol staff.
  14|                   Calls for service which will continue to rise, are affected by not only growth in our community, but the
  15|                   surrounding communities as well. Traffic too will continually increase and will have a direct effect on
  16|                   the amount of traffic accidents the police will have to investigate. Environmental factors also play a role
  17|                   in the motor vehicle accident picture. Incidents and arrests amounts are affected by social and economic
  18|                   tactors.
  19|                   1 again emphasize the important of keeping with current growth trends. Growth invariably plays a major
  20|                   role in police calls for service, arrests, incidents and motor vehicle accidents. The demands for police
  21|                   services will continue to rise and the police department will have to grow in kind.
  22|                   Calls for service have increased by 12.5% over last year. Incidents have increased by 16.7%. Incidents
  23|                   are cases that do not necessarily result in an Arrest, but require a report and investigation. Arrests are
  24|                   up by 33.7%, with motor vehicle accidents decreasing by 12.4%.
  25|                   Group A crimes against Persons has declined by 17% this year. These crimes include; Rape,
  26|                   Statutory Rape, Aggravated Assault, Simple Assault, Intimidation, Kidnapping/Abduction.
  27|                   Group A crimes against Property has also fallen by 17% this year. Crime against Property
  28|                   include; Burglary/Breaking & Entering, Larceny, Shoplifting, motor vehicle theft, Forgery, Fraud, stolen
  29|                   property, destruction of property, vandalism.
  30|                   Group A crimes against Society have increased by 14%. Crimes against Society include; Drug
  31|                   crimes, Pornography/Obscene Material, Weapons law violations.
  32|                   Group B crimes have increased by 22% this year. Group B crimes include; Bad checks,
  33|                   Disorderly conduct, Driving under the influence, Drunkenness, Family nonviolent offenses, Liquor Law
  34|                   Violations, Trespassing, and all other offenses.
  35|                   Lunenburg is a safe community to live in thanks in large part to citizen input. Reporting crime as soon as
  36|                   it's discovered is key to the safety of a Town such as ours. The Police cannot provide effective
  37|                   protection without the citizen involvement. I encourage every citizen to report crime as it occurs,
  38|                   without hesitation, as a timely response is vital to the successfully outcome of any police investigation.
  39|                   "See something, say something."

   2|                     Aro Estates. There will be a total of 122 houses built with construction being phased over a reasonable
   3|                     period of time to avoid overburdening the Town facilities and services all at once. As part of the
   4|                    approval the developer has agreed to donate approximately 40 acres of land to the Town for
   5|                     conservation purposes. Included as part of this donation will be a roadway extension within the
   6|                    subdivision(s) to provide public access to the boundary of the Town Forest.
   7|                    Construction continues on Massachusetts Avenue for an office and garage for the Lunenburg Water
   8|                               The Board also approved the construction and operation of a retail motor fuel outlet and
   9|                    convenience store with a drive-thru at 400 Electric Avenue.
  10|                    Various businesses asked for, and were approved for waiver requests from the site plan/special permit
  11|                    approval process. One of those businesses was Dick's Market Garden which received approval for
  12|                    construction of a new barn at 554 Lancaster Avenue for indoor washing and prepping vegetables for
  13|                    storage. This process had previously taken place outdoors.
  14|                    Through MRPC's District Local Technical Assistance Program a Residential & Commercial Permitting
  15|                    Guidebook was finalized. This Guidebook will facilitate permitting by providing easy-to-follow
  16|                    procedures that outline the permit review process for homeowners, property and business owners,
  17|                    developers, brokers and contractors.
  18|                    Under the Town's Capital Plan, the Town voted to approve $50,000 for FY 2020 for the Board to begin
  19|                    updating the 2002 Master Plan. The Master Plan is a policy document which outlines the community's
  20|                    vision for the future.
  21|                    In spring the Board identified economic development as an important topic to be addressed in the near
  22|                    future. To address this, the Planning Board began working to create an Economic Development
  23|                    Committee (EDC). This group would be composed of a combination of Town Officials, residents and
  24|                    business owners charged with providing advice, and guidance on the state of the business community
  25|                    in Lunenburg and ways that Town Government can support and sustain the local economy.
  26|                    At May 2019 Annual Town Meeting a Zoning Bylaw to regulate Recreational Adult Use Marijuana
  27|                    was passed. This Bylaw allows for no more than two (2) retail locations in the Town of Lunenburg.
  28|                    Retail locations are permitted in the Commercial and Office Park/Industrial Zoning Districts, as long as
  29|                    they are 250 feet away from Residential Zoning Districts and/or residential uses. The remaining types
  30|                    of Marijuana Establishments are permitted ONLY in the Office Park/Industrial District and must be
  31|                    located 500 feet from Residential Zoning Districts and/or residential uses.
  32|                    David Prokowiew stepped down from the Board; Kenneth Chenis finished out his term. Rhonda Lisio
  33|                    and Paula Bertram joined the Board.

  35|                   reached at 978-582-4147 ext. 5. Various forms are available online at https://lunenburgma.gov/ on the

   1|                   strategic objectives that will address district needs and realize the potential for the district I am proud to
   2|                   lead. One of the greatest challenges we face as a community is growing need and limited revenue. I
   3|                   want to acknowledge and express my gratitude for a collaborative approach to projecting and planning
   4|                   for current and future needs with the Town Manager, Heather Lemieux, and all the Town department
   5|                   heads. I appreciate the effort on the part of the School Committee, Board of Selectmen, and Finance
   6|                   Committee to strengthen communication and work collaboratively to problem-solve in order to meet the
   7|                   educational needs of the children of Lunenburg
   8|                   Because Lunenburg values education, we have two beautiful, modern facilities for teaching and
   9|                   learning; Lunenburg Middle-High School and Lunenburg Primary School. Turkey Hill Elementary
  10|                   School is now fifty years old and we know that the community will have to determine the future of this
  11|                   building in the next few years. A modern facility is but one factor in providing students with a quality
  12|                   education. Rigorous programming designed to meet the academic, behavioral, social and emotional
  13|                   needs of all of our students is foundational to a quality learning experience. It is also essential for the
  14|                   cultivation of a culture of safety, inclusivity, caring, and learning in our schools. At the heart of a
  15|                   who have the knowledge and skills requisite for effective instructional practice; a well-designed
  16|                   learning. Seamless integration of instructional technology to create engaging lessons, provide
  17|                   differentiated instruction and assessment, and facilitate analysis of achievement data to monitor the
  18|                   growth and support the success of all of our students is also essential.
  19|                   In October 2019, the School Committee approved the Strategy for Continuous Improvement which was
  20|                   developed to address the findings of my entry process last year. The Strategy is designed to meet our
  21|                   The Mission of the Lunenburg Public Schools is to provide all students with the skills, confidence,
  22|                   and passion for life-long learning so that they may find their own paths to successful careers, active
  23|                   citizenship, and rewarding lives.
  24|                   and Vision:
  25|                   The Lunenburg Public Schools is committed to becoming a leader in education by focusing on its
  26|                   values of attending to the personal well-being of its students, providing high-quality curricula and
  27|                   student-centered instruction for deeper learning at all levels, and promoting the development of
  28|                   attributes and skills necessary for continuous learning for all.
  29|                   The Strategy for Continuous Improvement is a living document which will be reviewed annually and
  30|                   adjusted as needed. The Strategic Objectives for the next three to five years are:
  31|                    Transforming Teaching and Learning: To ensure teaching and learning are student-centered and
  32|                   inclusive by: (1) establishing effective teams of educational leaders to accomplish the essential work for
  33|                   transforming practice, and (2) hiring and developing excellent educators who are continually learning
  34|                   and adapting practice to meet the needs of all students. (DSP Goals: 1, 2, 3, 5, {4, 73)
  35|                   Cultivating Climate & Culture: To create a culture that values data use and fosters continuous
  36|                   learning in a climate that is safe, caring, inclusive and reflective of our core values. (District Strategic
  37|                   Plan Goals 1, 6)

   1|                   connaent, ana capable young adults adequately preparea for tuture challenges. (District strategic plan
   2|                   Goals 1, 6)
   3|                   To cultivate district-wide leadership in key areas identified in the Strategy for Continuous
   4|                   Improvement, the Behavioral Health Advisory Council, Elementary Literacy Team, and Professional
   5|                   Development Committee were created in 2018-2019. In 2019-2020, we added a District Advisory
   6|                   Council and Equity Advisory Council. We expanded the District Leadership Team to include Food
   7|                   Service and Health Service, and created an Instructional Leadership Team focused on improving
   8|                   teaching and learning.
   9|                   In August 2019, the Instructional Leadership Team participated in a data workshop. With guidance
  10|                   from an instructional data consultant, the Team engaged in an analysis of the 2019 MCAS data as a
  11|                   team. We know we must continue to focus on curricular and instructional supports in order to
  12|                   increase the achievement and growth of our High Needs subgroup. Through the analysis, writing
  13|                   emerged as an area for additional focus at all grades. All principals worked with staff to include
  14|                   Student Learning Goals or Professional Practice Goals in writing in the Educator Evaluation Plans.
  15|                   The Team also reviewed the Entry Report of Findings and student survey results and determined that
  16|                   behavioral health (social emotional learning) was another key area in which our educators should
  17|                   focus their Professional Practice Goals.
  18|                            "One looks back with appreciation to the brilliant teachers, but with gratitude to

  22|                   As evidence of progress on our objectives, we have been revising and implementing our K-12 social

  24|                   Curriculum Framework; providing professional development in literacy at Turkey Hill Elementary
  25|                   School and Lunenburg Primary School. Lunenburg Primary School is participating in the second year

  27|                   Primary School we have continued with professional development in behavioral interventions and
  28|                   supports. The LHS staff are engaging in the NEASC accreditation process and have done much
  29|                   curriculum alignment as part of that process. The Behavioral Health Advisory Council and Wellness
  30|                   Committee have conducted student surveys and screenings at LMHS. This data helps us identify
  31|                   general student social and emotional needs and identify students who need intervention or supports.
  32|                   We have made improvements to our before and after school programs in order to provide additional

  21|                   Under the Department of Elementary and Secondary Education's accountability system, annual
  22|                   performance determinations for districts and schools are calculated using Indicators: English language
  23|                   arts (ELA), Mathematics, and Science achievement; ELA and Mathematics mean student growth
  24|                   percentile (SP); four-year cohort graduation rate, extended engagement rate, annual dropout rate;
  25|                   student progress towards attaining English language proficiency; chronic absenteeism, and percentage of
  26|                   11th & 12th graders completing advanced coursework.
  27|                   Indicators are weighted. Substantial weight for achievement, growth, English language proficiency, and
  28|                   graduation rate indicators is required by federal law. Federal law also requires that these indicators
  29|                   combined should have much greater weight than the additional indicators. Under the accountability
  30|                   system, the weighting of achievement and growth is based on ratios instead of percentages, maintaining
  31|                   the achievement to growth ratio of 3 to 1.
  32|                   Lastly, one of the greatest privileges as Superintendent is to honor a hard-working student with the
  33|                   Superintendent's Award. Every year the selection process is difficult as we have many hard-working,
  34|                   high-performing, community service oriented students. The recipient of the 2019 Superintendent's
  35|                   Award is Breda Page-Violette. Breda is the President of National Honor Society and the recipient of the
  36|                                                                         113

   1|                     Security Upgrades
   2|                     The district applied for a Safer Schools and Community Grant in April 2019 and was
   3|                     awarded $55,500 for 32 additional security cameras. The project was bid with Johnson
   4|                     Controls Security Solutions being awarded the work at $45,286.70. Work began in
   5|                     November 2019 and is expected to be complete by January 2020. The number of additional
   6|                     Cameras by scholary unco, 8. Addie ali Scory is be Turkey led for mentary Sterol,
   7|                     the grant funds.
   8|                     Water Testing
   9|                     In the second year of widespread water testing for lead and copper in the drinking water at
  10|                     all schools, in June 2019 lead was detected in a number of sources at Turkey Hill and
  11|                     Passios Elementary School. The source of the lead is believed to be solder from joining
  12|                     copper pipes. A number of high copper readings at Lunenburg Primary School suggest that
  13|                     the water district's corrosion control program needed to be reviewed. The water district has
  14|                     recommended that the ph level of the water needs to be raised. The teaching staff, parents
  15|                     and students have been made aware of the need to flush water sources at the start of the
  16|                     school day. Follow-up testing will occur at all schools in June 2020.
  17|                     Energy Savings
  18|                     The LED lighting conversion for Turkey Hill was completed in April 2019 with $36,233 in
  19|                     Green Community grant funds which paid $14,347.53 for materials and $21,855.47 to
  20|                     Duoba Electrical Contracting to install. The Green Community grant was supplemented by
  21|                     $1719 from the school budget and the remainder of $14,500 from a Unitil Corporation
  22|                     energy rebate.
  23|                     Capital Program
  24|                     The FY20 capital program continued to fund additional asbestos abatement at Turkey Hill
  25|                     Elementary School. In July 2019 Air Quality Experts, for $154,704.29, removed spray-on
  26|                     fireproofing above the ceiling on the south stairway and the second floor hallway of the
  27|                     classroom wing. This project included removal of the splined ceiling and the floor tiles in
  28|                     those areas. Turkey Hill Elementary School is expected to continue to have additional
  29|                     asbestos removal in the coming year with three first floor classrooms still to be abated. A
  30|                     request for $123,500 for FY21 was submitted into the town's capital planning process to
  31|                     abate rooms 102, 113 and 114. The school department also requested funds to begin air
  32|                     conditioning design for the Lunenburg Primary School classroom wing and the Turkey Hill
  33|                     administration area. Three split air conditioning systems would be added to cover the main
  34|                     office, guidance suite and nurse's office in July 2020 at an estimated cost of $28,000 if
  35|                     approvea.
  36|                     Respectfully submitted,
  37|                     John Londa
  38|                     Director of Facilities and Grounds.

  12|                                                     Lunenburg School Lunch Program
  13|                   Lunenburg School Food Service welcomes Zach Welton to our team. Zach is a Lunenburg High alumni,
  14|                   Class of 2019.
  15|                   We started a breakfast pilot in the Middle School on March 25, 2019 and continued through to the end
  16|                   of the school year. This pilot confirmed the need for breakfast in school and we were able to expand
  17|                  breakfast to all of our students at the start of school year 2019-2020. There is a link between breakfast
  18|                  and learning; students experience an improved learning environment. When breakfast is available at
  19|                   school students have improved attendance, decreased tardiness and discipline referrals.
  20|                  Breakfast is available Monday through Friday including early release days. Participation continues to
  21|                   grow. Please check out our webpage located at www.lunenburgschools.net/school-lunchfood-services
  22|                   for details regarding our breakfast and lunch program along with information from the National School
  23|                   Lunch Program. Meal benefits are an ongoing process and you can fill out an application at any time.
  24|                   The federal government requires a new application each school year. Free and Reduced meal
  25|                  applications can be found on our website page listed above.
  26|                   We are happy to have catered the boys' and girls' Varsity and JV soccer banquet again this fall. What a
  27|                  pleasure it is to see the student athletes and their families in this setting. Thank you to Angela Ashton,
  28|                  Dennis Mannon and the Soccer Boosters for supporting our program while celebrating theirs. Check out
  29|                  the catering menu on our webpage and let us cater your next event.
  30|                  Respectfully Submitted
  31|                  Nadine Lorenzen
  32|                  Food Service Director

===PAGE 123=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|                   The October 1, 2019 student enrollment reported to the Department of Elementary and Secondary
   2|                   Education was 421 students. Currently, our Preschool program services 45 three and four year old
   3|                   students. We have six Kindergarten classes servicing 123 students. Class sizes in our Kindergartens are
   4|                   at 20-21 students. This year we have six first grade classrooms servicing 147 students with class sizes
   5|                   ranging between 23-25. Five second grade classrooms have 119 students with class sizes averaging at
   6|                   24 students.
   7|                   Our professional development has centered on literacy instruction and Social Emotional Learning this
   8|                   year. In early October we received notification from Governor Charles D. Baker that the school was
   9|                   awarded a FY20 Early Grades Literacy grant of $37,705. This grant funded teacher participation in an
  10|                   extensive professional development opportunity that included three statewide literacy institutes and
  11|                   follow-up regional meetings. Along with a consultant who works with teachers to provide clear, specific
  12|                   examples of how the early literacy aligns with our goals and supports the staff in a coaching model.
  13|                   We explored the development of writing in young children (birth-9); the role language plays in literacy;
  14|                   and the content of the Massachusetts ELA/Literacy Framework Standards for Language. Teachers
  15|                   discussed strategies to engage early grades students in writing, and thinking about the content they are
  16|                   learning in order to write with purpose and reason. The grant team shared information with grade level
  17|                   teams throughout the year. Our second grade team has worked with Mary Canner from Teachers21 and
  18|                  they have focused on implementing, Lucy Calkins: Units for Study for teaching reading, this Readers'
  19|                   Workshop model helps teachers provide their students with instruction, opportunities for practice, and
  20|                   concrete doable goals to help them meet and exceed any set of high standards. The main goal of this
  21|                   design is to prepare students for any reading and writing task they will face and to turn kids into life-
  22|                  long, confident readers and writers who display agency and independence.
  23|                   Our staff works to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and supportive. In September
  24|                   and November all of the Primary staff participated in two full days of professional development on
  25|                   Positive Behavior Intervention and Supports (PBIS). Positive Behavioral Interventions and Supports
  26|                   (PBIS) is an evidence-based three-tiered framework for improving and integrating all of the data,
  27|                   systems, and practices affecting student outcomes every day. The system supports all students through a
  28|                   multi-tiered framework and this year's focus was the Universal Tier (Tier 1). We have worked with
  29|                   Maureen Gilman of Seaside Educational Consultants to develop universal school-wide positive
  30|                   expectations that are practiced throughout our school community. We have worked on establishing and
  31|                   teaching the school wide expectations along with having a continuum of encouraging expected behavior
  32|                   and discouraging problem behaviors. This system is aligning nicely with The Anchors of Emotional
  33|                   Intelligence, a whole-school/district-wide approach to teaching social emotional learning which the staff
  34|                   has been trained in and is using to help students recognize, understand, describe, communicate and
  35|                   regulate their emotions.
  36|                   The Primary School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to Common assessments are
  37|                   insure that all students have the services they need to be successful.
  38|                   completed in the fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K-2 in the areas of reading, writing,
  39|                   and math. These common assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and the
  40|                   progress of students. Struggling students are identified for additional support in Reading, Math, Speech,
  41|                   Occupational and Physical Therapy.

   1|                                                                      Shawn Sproul
   2|                                                  Turkey Hill Elementary School
   3|                   Our fourth year as Turkey Hill Elementary has gotten off to a strong start. There are currently 364
   4|                   students, with 120 third graders, 124 fourth graders, and 120 fifth graders. The average homeroom size
   5|                   is 24. Our MCAS data from the spring of 2019 indicates we are meeting or exceeding expectations
   6|                   above state averages in the following areas: Grade 3 Math at 55%, and Grade 5 Science at 50%. There
   7|                   are teaching teams of 2 as well as self-contained classrooms at each grade. Special area classes include
   8|                   art, music, PE, library, technology, and computer skills.
   9|                   Teachers are engaging in a variety of trainings. 4h and 5th   grade teachers have received professional
  10|                   development in Calkins Units of Study, Reader's Workshop and 3'd grade teachers in Writer's
  11|                   Workshop.
  12|                   Math teachers have participated in trainings for IXL math and using Cuisenaire rods for number sense.
  13|                   A full day of professional development in November was dedicated to behavioral health. In December,
  14|                   a number of us visited a charter school in Cambridge with laudatory MCAS scores and others went to
  15|                   Framingham State for a math conference.
  16|                   We are offering many after school activities for students this winter. About 100 students signed up for a
  17|                   variety of goings-on including cooking: they've experimented with graham cracker art and ice-cream in
  18|                   a bag; winter wonderland: sledding and snowman creations on the hill behind LMHS; crafts: sock
  19|                   puppets; science: fake boogers, baking soda and vinegar explosions, and bouncy balls; drama club and
  20|                   chess. We had the Girls on the Run program in the fall with 18 participants, coached by two third grade
  21|                   teachers.
  22|                   Fifth graders spent a week in Beckett, MA enjoying the Nature's Classroom program. The Bridges
  23|                   program introduced students to several senior citizen volunteers who came in to share their life
  24|                   experiences and learn about the younger generation. The six week program culminated in a walking
  25|                   field trip to the Eagle House in December. Grade 5 teachers lead regular walking field trips to the public
  26|                   library every month so students can become good community members with the responsibility of
  27|                   borrowing and returning library books.
  28|                   In June the students attended a Worcester Bravehearts baseball game at Hanover Park and enjoyed the
  29|                   activities for Baseball in Education Day. We finished the year with our first "completion ceremony" for
  30|                   fifth grade students to celebrate the end of their elementary years.
  31|                   The TIGER performances from Plymouth State University provided a program to Grades 3 and 4 in
  32|                   December about how to be a good friend and how to speak up for others. This was underwritten by a
  33|                   generous grant from the Maki Foundation. Fourth graders will welcome the Boston Museum of Science
  34|                   out in the spring to learn about vibrations and how changes in their frequency and amplitude result in
  35|                   different kinds of sound. Third graders took a trip to Monty Tech for a performance of The Nutcracker.

  15|                   3rd graders are introduced to instruments through recorder instruction. All students also have the

  17|                   As an elementary school we understand that our mission extends far beyond the academic. We model,
  18|                   teach and promote the CARES qualities of civility, achievement, respect, effort, and sportsmanship. We

  21|                   age appropriate picture books at each grade to introduce students to diverse cultures and expand their
  22|                   understanding of the world. We teach responsible digital citizenship in an era where soon they will be
  23|                   inundated with social media. We celebrate the positive role models that exist in our school community

   1|                   embraces the philosophy of educating the whole child academically, socially, and emotionally. This
   2|                   philosophy is a means by which all students become positive community members that possess the
   3|                   skills to be successful in high school and beyond.
   4|                   Lunenburg Middle School is in its fourth year as a co-located middle-high school serving grades six
   5|                   through eight in a beautiful facility. Effective, student centered middle schools embrace
   6|                   interdisciplinary teams of teachers that enable teachers to work with smaller groups of students. Smaller
   7|                   groups of students allow teachers to learn and engage students in their preferred learning style, making
   8|                   them more confident and successful.
   9|                   Our effective middle school model is student centered and provides opportunities to explore a variety of
  10|                   different interests and talents through special area and enrichment classes and extracurricular activities.
  11|                   One of our goals is for students to discover something they are passionate about and want to continue
  12|                   exploring as they move through high school and beyond. We will continue to seek out opportunities for
  13|                   students that foster critical thinking and problem solving skills that prepare them for high school and
  14|                   beyond or what their passion may lead them to.

===PAGE 130=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                    be sta de cro percent, up cla in ule slale. As you can see, our
   2|                    scores in grade eight Science Technology and Engineering are 20% higher than the state average.
   3|                    Although we are proud of our students' performance, we continue to analyze data in our monthly results
   4|                    faculty meetings designed to improve our instruction for all students. Our faculty has identified areas of
   5|                    improvement based upon the data and implemented strategies to improve their instructional practices
   6|                    that will help students succeed.
   7|                    We continue to utilize intervention and power blocks during the school day to meet the needs of each
   8|                    individual student. These blocks of instructional time that have been built into our master schedule to
   9|                   allow teachers time to work with students at their own individual skill level. The philosophy is to
  10|                    challenge students who are high academic achievers as well as strengthen skills for students who have
  11|                    identified weaknesses. Teachers are able to work with students in a small group to answer individual
  12|                    questions or provide more individualized support. We design this support model to allow students to
  13|                    feel more confident, thus, perform better in the classroom. We will continue to refine our intervention
  14|                    block times to best meet the needs of our students. One option for students during this intervention
  15|                    block is the use of our web-based program called IXL. IXL allows students to work at their own skill
  16|                   level, build confidence and moved forward with work that is aligned with the state standards. IXL
  17|                    allows teachers a tool for true differentiation for every student allowing them to work and grow at their
  18|                    own pace and level.
  19|                    Lunenburg Middle School continues to offer enrichment blocks for all students. Enrichment classes
  20|                   provide students a block of time in which they receive instruction in the areas of developmental health
  21|                   and guidance. Students also receive instruction in physical education, art, and music classes that allows
  22|                   them to explore their strengths and talents. Students also have the opportunity to sign up for and
  23|                   participate in band and chorus during this block. This enrichment block is in addition to the traditional
  24|                   special area classes students receive, and this block offers students additional physical movement
  25|                   throughout the day, additional opportunities for hands on activities and more exposure to the arts,
  26|                   library and technology throughout the school day.
  27|                    We make a positive impact on the students we serve by offering a variety of different programs
  28|                   developed to promote social and emotional growth, self-awareness, and community service. We will
  29|                   continue to utilize Developmental Design program to deliver our social emotional curriculum. Students
  30|                   meet with teachers in small groups to teach social responsibility and problem solving, as well as
  31|                   developing empathy and promoting positive self-esteem and strong teacher/student relationships. Our
  32|                   social emotional programs have been developed to allow students to showcase their talents and abilities,
  33|                   grow and improve as individuals, and promote community outreach for the younger generation.
  34|                   We also provide students the opportunity to direct their own learning with a program called Genius
  35|                   Hour. Genius Hour fosters a passion for learning back into the curriculum by having students facilitate
  36|                   their own learning based on their interests.
  37|                   Lunenburg Middle School continues to provide high quality educational services and supports that
  38|                   ensure student success and promote a passion for lifelong learning. The key components to our success
  39|                   is our talented faculty committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and community
  40|                   who support our teaching and learning, and most of all, our students who inspire us through their
  41|                   commitment to learning. Together we continue to make the difference in the lives of students.

===PAGE 138=== (ocr, 40 lines)
   1|                    comprehensive high school, our students are also learning a valuable trade - skills they will carry with
   2|                    them for a lifetime. These vocational skills may provide opportunities for our graduates to work their
   3|                    way through college; they may open the door to new, related career pathways, or they may be the
   4|                    foundation for a lifelong career. The education and training they receive at Monty Tech will open doors
   5|                    and allow them to choose a college and/or career pathway that will define their future.
   6|                    Monty Tech academic programs continue to serve students at all levels well. This year, students
   7|                    completed the Next Generation MCAS, and earned commendable passing rates: English Language Arts
   8|                    99%o, Mathematics 97%, and Biology 99%. Monty Tech students exceeded the state averages in all 3
   9|                    subjects. In addition, great strides were made in our relatively new Advanced Placement programs,
  10|                    with more than 60% of test takers earning a score of 3 or better. Finally, an all-new science program
  11|                    was fully integrated in FY19 - Biomedical Sciences. This rigorous sequence of courses was met with
  12|                    enthusiasm trom students and statt, so plans to expand this Project Lead the Way programming are
  13|                    underway. Our talented academic faculty continue to refine curriculum, incorporate new instructional
  14|                    strategies, and challenge every student to reach new levels, and we couldn't be prouder of the results.
  15|                    And while we are certainly proud of our academic and vocational training programs, a Monty Tech
  16|                    experience would not be complete without participating in meaningful community service. Last year ViM Lay Tech
  17|                    we were honored to once again host the Wreaths Across America Convoy with a remembrance
  18|                    ceremony as it made its way down to Arlington National Cemetery. As the convoy left the school
  19|                    parking lot and hundreds of American flags waved, I was reminded once again of the value of a Monty
  20|                    lech education. For a moment, our students were taken out of the classrooms and shops and asked to
  21|                    think about what this convoy meant, where it was heading and how they were a small part of the
  22|                    ceremony. They were asked to consider service, and through the waves and cheers it was clear that
  23|                   Monty Tech students knew and understood the meaning behind that experience. It's not often that high
  24|                    school students are asked to stop and think about others and how they might contribute to another
  25|                    person's well-being. But at Monty Tech, our students are not only familiar with the concept, they are
  26|                    committed to community service. Whether it is building a carriage house for the NEADS program,
  27|                    designing and building dugouts for area Little League programs, or ensuring handicap accessibility in
  28|                    dated town offices, our staff and students are proud to serve.
  29|                    While students and instructors across the school continue to demonstrate creativity, innovation, and
  30|                   leadership, school leaders continue to keep an eye on emerging trends in vocational education. As we
  31|                    prepare these talented students to make important life choices, we are reminded that in the end, the
  32|                    choice is theirs to make: college or career or both. It is our job to support their aspirations and to
  33|                    educate and train them to achieve at the highest possible level. On behalf of our talented educators and
  34|                    administrators, who remain focused on delivering the highest quality academic and vocational-technical
  35|                   education possible, I am delighted to present the District's 2018-2019 annual report to you, providing a
  36|                   snapshot of the wonderful experiences happening on a day-to-day basis here at Monty Tech.
  37|                   Respectfully submitted,
  38|                        Chile My Manity
  39|                   Sheila M. Harrity, Ed.D., Superintendent-Director

   8|                   Throughout 2018-2019, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents
   9|                   and community members to learn about and visit the school.
  10|                   In October 2018, approximately 450 district eighth graders participated in the annual "Tour Day"
  11|                   event. Students toured our twenty-one vocational-technical areas and learned about the school's
  12|                   challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and extracurricular programs.
  13|                   Career Awareness Night offered interested students the opportunity to return in the evening with their
  14|                   family members to further explore the facilities and speak with staff members.
  15|                   The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance to
  16|                   visit Monty Tech after school and participate in hands
  17|                   on learning experiences across a variety of vocational-technical areas. The program continued to
  18|                   attract a large number of students during the 2018-2019 school year, serving approximately 700 area
  19|                   students.
  20|                   Class of 2019 Awards
  21|                   Members of the Class of 2019 were awarded approximately $200,000 in scholarships, tools, and
  22|                   equipment. The Monty Tech Foundation generously provided $36,000 in scholarships to graduating
  23|                   seniors, ranging in amounts of $500 to $2,000. The Foundation also awarded $3,250 to the Practical
  24|                   Nursing graduates. Once again, local and state organizations, as well as generous individuals,
  25|                   continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech graduates in the form of financial
  26|                   donations. The School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates are grateful for this support.
  27|                   Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost of
  28|                   higher education. Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits through a
  29|                   number of articulation agreements with public and private colleges across the country.
  30|                   Approximately 38% of the graduating class of 2019 reported plans to enroll at a 4-ear college/
  31|                   university upon graduation. By earning college credits while still in high school, these students will
  32|                   save both time and money as they pursue advanced educational programs.

  12|                    Academic Achievement
  13|                    During the spring of 2019, students at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School continued
  14|                    to demonstrate high academic achievement,        earning commendable scores on the MCAS in English
  15|                    Language Arts, Mathematics, and Biology. This year, students completed the Next Generation MCAS
  16|                   - a computer-based test that is designed to give students a clear signal on whether they are on track for
  17|                    college and career readiness.   The new standards are more rigorous than the standards for the Legacy
  18|                    MCAS. Monty Tech's passing rate on the English Language Arts was 99%, Mathematics 97%,
  19|                    Biology 99%. Monty Tech students exceeded the state averages in all 3 subjects. With regard to our
  20|                    Accountability status, Monty Tech has made "Substantial Progress Toward Meeting our Targets."
  21|                    Monty Tech is committed to providing students with rigorous STEM learning opportunities, and so in
  22|                    recent years, the curriculum has been expanded to include Advanced Placement Programs (two English
  23|                    courses, two mathematics courses, two science courses, and one computer science course) and teachers
  24|                    have participated in meaningful, high-quality professional development which has enabled us to
  25|                    successfully implement two Project Lead the Way biomedical courses.

  10|                   Vocational Projects in the District Communities
  11|                   Unlike students in traditional, comprehensive high schools, students at Monty Tech are asked to put
  12|                   their education into practice on a daily basis. Students across the twenty-one different vocational-
  13|                   technical education programs are building homes, reconstructing damaged properties, repairing service
  14|                   vehicles, making walkways more accessible, and performing countless community services.
  15|                   The 2018-2019 school year was a busy one for our vocational educators, as each trade aimed to provide
  16|                   practical, hands- on learning experiences for our students, while helping to improve each of the eighteen member communities.
  17|                   Auto Body Collision Repair Technology: The Monty Tech Auto Body shop benefited from new
  18|                   instructional equipment this year. A new silicon bronze MIG welder was added to the program, so that
  19|                   students continue to be trained in the industry's latest technologies. The shop floor was coated with a
  20|                   new epoxy flooring, and a new lift was installed and relocated to replace the old lift, updating and
  21|                   enhancing the instructional space. Three new Vacuum sanders were installed to keep the dust in the
  22|                   shop down, and four new shop benches were installed.          In addition to the 80+ community service
  23|                   projects completed last year, students and instructors painted a sign for Hubbardston Council on Aging,
  24|                   as well as one of Monty Tech's vans. All Seniors received their EPA 6 H Certificate, I-Car Pro Level 1
  25|                   in Refinishing and Non- Structural Repair Certification, and a SEM Plastic Repair Certificate.
  26|                   Seniors and two Juniors earned co-op placements, and one Senior traveled to Louisville, KY, competing
  27|                   in the SkillsUSA national competition in June 2018. A 2017 graduate of the program traveled to Russia
  28|                  to compete in the SkillsUSA World competition, where he placed an impressive 14"              (Total student
  29|                   enrollment: 63)
  30|                   Automotive Technology: As in past years, the Monty Tech Automotive Technology program continues
  31|                  to service a variety of vehicles. Over 400 vehicles were serviced and repaired during the 2018-2019
  32|                   school year, including vehicles brought in by faculty, staff, public, school and students. Students
  33|                   pertormed repairs that will prepare them for the workplace, such as timing belts, electronic diagnostics,
  34|                   intake manifold gaskets, brake work and various other technical repairs. The program was awarded
  35|                  approximately $200,000 from the FY20 Massachusetts Capital Skills grant program, and will use those
  36|                   funds to provide a more comprehensive electrical and electronics training curriculum. Two Snap-On
  37|                   NC3 certification programs have been added, including digital multimeters and scan tool application.
  38|                   Nine students earned co-op placements throughout the school year, which is a testament to the strength
  39|                   of the program. The Monty Tech Automotive Technology program continues to be the area's premier
  40|                   workforce pipeline for automotive dealerships and businesses.

  31|                   Co-operative Education and Student Placement
  32|                   The Cooperative Education Program is an extension of the student's technical education that combines
  33|                                 instruction with on-the-job-training. The Co-op Program
  34|                   environment. All students are eligible to participate in Co-op Program, provided they satisfy state and opportunity to further develop academic, technical and employability skills in an industry work
  35|                   school grade, attendance and performance requirements. All Co-op students have completed the OSHA
  36|                   recognized Career Safe online health and safety course.
  37|                   The Cooperative Education Program is beneficial for both student and employer. For the student, it is a chance to gain on-the-job training that improves both their technical and employability skills.
  38|                    Additionally, making industry connections enhances post-graduate employment opportunities. The Co-
  39|                    op Program allows students to earn while they learn.

  12|                   Employer benefits include addressing workforce needs in an efficient manner. Co-op students provide a pool
  13|                   of temporary and potential full-time employees who are already trained, thus reducing employment training costs. Co-op
  14|                   work hours may be tailored to suit the needs of partnering employers. During the 2018-2019 school year, approximately
  15|                   22.4% of qualified students (juniors and seniors) earned Co-op placements, working, learning and applying their
  16|                   technical skills related in area businesses.
  17|                   Student Support Services
  18|                   During the 2018-2019 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District
  19|                   provided special services to approximately three hundred students - measuring progress of over two
  20|                   hundred students on Individual Education Plans (IEPs) and just under one hundred students adhering to
  21|                   individualized Section 504 plans. While the Student Support Services (SSS) Department encompasses
  22|                   special education, the department provides support and is available to all Monty Tech students.
  23|                   The department includes a full-time nursing staff that administers medications, performs state-mandated
  24|                   health screening exams, and provides when necessary, health information to the special education team
  25|                   for a student's IEP meeting.       The department benefits from a full-time school social worker that
  26|                   participates in departmental meetings and assists students who have needs concerning finances, family
  27|                   issues, homelessness, maternity, health issues, and proper food and clothing. The school is also
  28|                   fortunate to have on staff a full-time psychologist, whose role it is to evaluate all students referred for an
  29|                   initial evaluation or who require a three-year re-evaluation. In addition, we have a full-time speech
  30|                   language pathologist, who is available to assist students with disabilities, assess these students and
  31|                   consult with teachers. Our students also have access to the services of a full-time adjustment counselor
  32|                   and part-time school psychologist. All of these individuals are available for scheduled counseling
  33|                   sessions, mental health emergency treatment, medical/mental health re-entry and transition support
  34|                   services, as well as crisis intervention.
  35|                   The school's Director of Student Support Services oversees the District's Special Education Program,
  36|                   which is reviewed annually in May, in accordance with regulatory requirements. The comprehensive
  37|                   review and evaluation are done in collaboration with the Parent Advisory Council, and the results of the
  38|                   evaluation are used to improve the special education procedures and programs in place at Montachusett
  39|                   Regional Vocational Technical School.
  40|                                                                        146

===PAGE 149=== (ocr, 37 lines)
   1|                   and supports for instructors. The District's Instructional Technologist and four academic instructors
   2|                   attended the annual Schoology Conterence, with the goal of identifying new solutions, technologies, and
   3|                   best practices that will enhance the school's technology-driven approach to education.
   4|                   To support these expanded technology needs, Monty Tech successfully completed a number of necessary
   5|                   infrastructure upgrades, using federal e-rate dollars totaling $116,511. The school's external bandwidth
   6|                   speeds have been increased to 2.5 GB and internal bandwidth to 10GB to better ensure continued
   7|                   connectivity and speeds appropriate with a high-use learning community.
   8|                   Marine Corps JROTC
   9|                   The Monty Tech Marine Corps Junior Reserve Officer Training Corps (MCJROTC) had a productive
  10|                   2018-2019 school year. The Cadet Corps stood a formal Inspector General inspection and achieved a
  11|                   "Mission Ready" grade, and due to outstanding performance throughout the year, Monty Tech's Marine
  12|                   Corps Junior Reserve Officers' Training Corps program was designated as a Marine Corps Honor
  13|                   School. Selection for the Marine Corps Honor School means that the school's Marine Corps JROTC
  14|                   program was in the top 15% of the 68 other programs in Region 1. Region 1 includes schools from the mid-Atlantic and Northeast U.S. The selection is based on several criteria including:
  15|                   • Inspector General's inspection results
  16|                   • Number of cadets in the program
  17|                   • Number of community service hours conducted
  18|                   • Number of public affairs events attended by cadets
  19|                   • Number of academic awards presented to cadets
  20|                   • Participation in drill team competitions, marksmanship competitions, physical fitness competitions,
  21|                        and field trips that support the growth and development of the cadet
  22|                    Because of this distinction, Monty Tech MJROTC instructors are allowed to nominate up to six cadets
  23|                   to each of the three service academies — U.S. Naval Academy, U.S. Military Academy and U.S. Air
  24|                   Force Academy. This is the 8 time the program has earned this distinction since 2007.
  25|                    The Monty Tech JROTC Cadet Corps was honored to once again host the Wreaths Across America
  26|                    Convoy with a remembrance ceremony as it made its way down to Arlington National Cemetery.
  27|                    The Cyber Security Team received national recognition by securing a place to compete in the Air Force
  28|                    Association's National Cyber Security Competition held in Baltimore, MD, for the ninth consecutive
  29|                   yech placed spetition feld hegan with over 1900 hundred tams batting for just 13 seals, and Monty in the highly competitive field in the all service division.
  30|                    The 2018-2019 Corps of Cadets completed over 9,600 hours of community service, conducting a major
  31|                   food drive with United Way, completing a 10 mile March A Thon that raised $21,000 in support of
  32|                    NEADS, and spending five weekends working with the local Salvation Army helping to raise over
  33|                    $21,000 for needy families. The Monty Tech Cadet Corps provided 31 Color Guards for local civic and
  34|                    veteran ceremonies, and supported 19 community service projects throughout North Central
  35|                    Massachusetts.    Through civic engagement and veteran affiliation, our Cadets received more than
  36|                    $180,000 dollars in scholarships.

   1|                   small business partnerships, and the number and quality of personal enrichment classes - sewing,
   2|                   knitting, acrylic painting and glass fusing, to name a few. Catalog distribution has more than doubled,
   3|                   from 80,000 to 170,000 copies, and the relatively new online registration feature continues to add value
   4|                   to our students and instructors.
   5|                   In Spring 2019, we offered an all-new Oil Burner Technician certification prep course. Students in the
   6|                   newly established the 120- hour program trained on new boilers and burners, and upon completion
   7|                   earned the opportunity to sit for the oil burner technician state licensing exam. This course provides our
   8|                   community members with a high quality, affordable entry into a viable career pathway. We have also
   9|                   added an EPA 608 certification course to benefit students in the HVAC industry.
  10|                   Our medical course offerings now include Pharmacy Technician, Certified Medical Billing and Coding,
  11|                   EKG, Phlebotomy, Certified Nurse Aide, Patient Care Tech, and Medical Terminology.
  12|                   The continued success and sustainability of an adult education program is based on hiring quality instructo
  13|                   rs and meeting the needs of the community and supporting workforce development. Our goals in the
  14|                   coming year include identifying additional training opportunities to meet the needs of the regional
  15|                   workforce, provide affordable and meaningful training opportunities to our nontraditional student
  16|                   population, and expanding programming to include off-site, daytime training opportunities across the
  17|                   Practical Nursing Program
  18|                   The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program is designed to prepare graduates to practice safely and
  19|                   ethically in a caring manner for patients who are experiencing common variations in health status in
  20|                     heal in caring mans.
  21|                   On June 27, 2019 a graduating class of 28 students completed the Practical Nursing Program and
  22|                   entered the nursing profession. The class achieved a pass rate of 96%, with 27 of the 28 graduates
  23|                   passing the NCLEX-PN exam (National Council Licensure Examination for Practical Nurses). One
  24|                   graduate will pursue the examination in the coming months.
  25|                   district as Licensed Practical Nurses in various health care settings, such as long-term care, sub-acute
  26|                   care, mental health/ substance abuse facilities, physician's offices and correctional medicine.
  27|                   The Monty Tech Practical Nursing Program continues to strengthen the "LPN to BSN (Bachelor of
  28|                   Science in Nursing) Bridge" relationship with Fitchburg State University: Several 2019 graduates are
  29|                   pursuing seats in the LPN to BSN program at FSU and will be continuing their education to the
  30|                   Bachelors in Nursing. Many class applicants cited their attraction to Monty Tech as the opportunity to
  31|                   complete a ten-month accelerated program and then bridge to Fitchburg State University. In the 5-year
  32|                   history of the Bridge Program, all of Monty Tech's Practical Nursing graduates reaching graduation at
  33|                   FSU have passed their RNNCLEX exam.
  34|                   Monty Tech Practical Nursing students are now completing patient scenarios in the Sim Lab on a
  35|                   weekly basis in Terms 2, 3, and 4. The Faculty Sim team has implemented National League of Nursing
  36|                   (NLN) patient scenarios and authored several others consistent with our curriculum frameworks, and
  37|                   have also utilized the Sim lab setting to instruct students in developing nursing skills that they may not
  38|                    be experiencing in the clinical setting.
